Addendum  XI 
Funding proposal package f or FP 236 
 
Summary  
This addendum contains the following seven parts:  
a) A funding proposal titled "Basin Approach for Livelihood Sustainability through 
Adaptation Strategies (BALSAS) "; 
b) No-objection letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal 
point(s);  
c) Environmental and social report(s) disclosure;  
d) Secretariat ’s assessment;  
e) Independent Technical Advisory Panel ’s assessment;  
f) Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's 
assessment; and  
g) Gender documentation.

Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template  
● Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross -
reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.  
● Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details 
provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be 
reflected in all relevant documents.  
● The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not 
exceed 60. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual 
service standard time.  
● The recommended font is Arial, size 11.  
● Under the  GCF Information Disclosure Policy , project and programme funding proposals 
will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board, 
subject to the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. 
Accredited Entities are asked to fill out information on disclosure in section G.4.    
 
Please submit the completed proposal to: 
fundingproposal@gcfund.org 
Please use the following name convention for the file name: 
“FP-[Accredited Entity Short Name] -[Country/Region] -[YYYY/MM/DD]”

 A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)  
 
The Balsas Basin is one of the two regions in Mexico with the highest level of climate vulnerability. This is exacerbated 
by high levels of poverty and marginalization across the rural population. These conditions are worsened by negative 
dynamics which ar e eroding vulnerable rural livelihoods and productive systems, which have yet to adapt to climate 
variability and change. Severely increasing temperatures, anomalous and more intense rainfall seasons,  and longer 
dry seasons as a result from Climate Change and the El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO) continue to aggravate social 
vulnerability in the Balsas Basin by reducing the productivity of agricultural and natural resource- based livelihoods, and 
advers ely affecting already dwindling ecosystem services. With the length of the dry season anticipated to increase, 
areas of the Basin with high annual precipitation will experience shorter but more intense rainfall events. This implies 
that flooding, crop dama ge and soil erosion will be compounded in the rainy season while extended droughts may be 
more frequent during the rest of the year. Furthermore, in already arid and semi -arid zones, the lengthening of the dry 
season will imply a decrease in the productivi ty of the vegetation.3 Finally, increasingly dry soils will take longer to 
recover their humidity, so growing seasons will shorten, reducing crop yields.4 If landscapes and ecosystems are not 
restored and their capabilities to buffer these extremes improved, the positive changes in the wet season do not carry 
over to the dry season because excess water is not stored.5 Addressing these problems is, however, restricted because 
currently local capacities are insufficient and institutional modalities do not consider climate change adaptation criteria 
in prioritizing forest landscape restoration incentive payments in the most vulnerable areas and communities.  
 
This cross- cutting project (80% adaptation, 20% mitigation) aims to increase the climate resilience of the most 
vulnerable communities and ecosystems, and reduce emissions from production systems in the Balsas Project Area, 
via: i.) operationalizing the us e of climate change criteria in current institutional modalities for the prioritization of rural 
development incentives  through a community -centric bottom- up approach; ii.) investing in the climate- sensitive 
management of ecosystems, and climate- resilient productive systems and value chains for adaptation and mitigation 
benefits; and iii.) supporting a climate information and risk management system, and the systematic management of 
knowledge and lessons learnt . 
 The objective of the project is to enable community -level investment in adaptation and mitigation initiatives, through the 
climate -focused transformation of rural development  incentives for the conservation of forest resources  and t he 
strengthening of  local institutions and capacities  to bring under sustainable management, conservation or restoration 
324,578 ha of land, contributing to Target 5 on the Updated Strategic Plan of the GCF for 2024- 2027 This objective will 
also be achieved by the project’s strong focus on bottom- up decision- making and value chain development , ensuring  
social inclusion, benefiting and empowering women, youth and indigenous peoples. The proposed project will shift 
current sectoral rural development support allocation systems used by rural development institutions to a  new 
integrated territorial development approach which:  
 
● Uses climate change- related criteria for selecting and prioritizing the most vulnerable beneficiaries, 
interventions, and sites.  
● Strengthens local watershed governance and participation of women, youth, and indigenous peoples at the 
sub-basin level to create incentives, reinforce inter institutional coordination, and foster a bottom- up approach for the 
sustainable use, management, and conservation of crucial ecosystems and ecosystem services to be piloted in the 
Balsas Project Area and replicated at national level. As part of the project’s bottom- up approach, the participation of 
Agrarian Communities and Producer Organizations in value chains of timber and non- timber forest products (NTFPs) 
will also be strengthened, with the aim of  increasing the incomes of the most vulnerable households, thereby building 
their adaptive capacity.  
● Disburses incentive payments for forest ecosystem and landscape conservation multi -annually under a 
progress -based payment system, specifically rewarding performance of adaptation and mitigation initiatives. The multi -
annual approach and annual evaluation of progress was first piloted by CONAFOR for Payment for Environmental 
Services ( PES)  schemes only but will be replicated to  other forestry development incentive schemes.  
 The National Forestry Commission of Mexico ( Comisión Nacional Forestal  CONAFOR) , Nacional Financiera S.N.C., 
Banca de Desarrollo NAFI N and the Ministry of Finance (HACIENDA)  are the Executing Entities , whereby CONAFOR 
will undertake all operational responsibility for the implementation of the Project and NAFIN and HACIENDA will only 
channel funds . NAFIN  is accredited as  Direct Access Entity (DAE) to the GC F and through its  participation in this 
Project , it will strengthen its capacities and experience to prepare and execute GCF co- financed climate change 
adaptation and mitigation projects  in the future.  Thus, the Accredited Entity IFAD, due to its long- term presence in the 
region and its multi -year international experience with several similar projects with the GCF, wants to capitalize on its 
previous successful experiences with GCF projects and provide innovative solutions to this project and strengthen the 
capacities of the partner organizations involved.

 B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION  
B.1. Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)  
General context  
1. The Balsas Basin (Hydrological -Administrative Region RHA IV) and part of the South Pacific 
Hydrological -Administrative Region (RHA V) adjacent to it (henceforth “Project Area” PA) encompasses the states 
of Guerrero, Jalisco, México, Michoacán, Morelos, Oaxaca, Puebla, and T laxcala, which together cover 695 
municipalities in south- west Mexico. The predominant climate is warm subhumid, with an average annual 
temperature of 22 °C and a regional average annual rainfall of 1,027 mm, varying considerably depending on the 
orography. The highest rainfall occurs in the Sierra Madre del Sur and the Transverse Neo- volcanic System, 
while the Inter -montane depressions suffer from dry to arid climate. The rainy season occurs from June to 
September, w hich concentrates more than 80% of the annual precipitation6. The PA is one of the two regions in 
Mexico with the highest level of climatic vulnerability7..The Balsas basin has a hydraulic infrastructure of great 
relevance at the national level, especially for hydropower generation, which uses 78% of the water volume 
produced in the basin. The Balsas basin hosts the second largest dam in Mexico: “El Infiernil lo”, with a storage 
capacity of 12,500 hm3.  
2. The population of the PA is vulnerable to the impacts of climate change because of its high levels of 
poverty and low social development relative to the rest of Mexico, which is reflected in significant deficiencies in health, food, education, social security, housing, drinking w ater and basic sanitation, as well as limited income, 
access to decent employment and lack of productive options. This situation has generated adverse land use 
practices which threaten the sustainability of natural resources and the most vulnerable livelih oods , putting high 
pressure on soils, ecosystems, and ecosystem services, particularly water regulation. L
ack of employment 
opportunities in rural areas disproportionally affects rural women, youth and indigenous peoples. Extreme poverty 
is primarily, though not exclusively, a rural phenomenon. The concentration of poverty shows a persistent pattern: 
it is mai nly located in the southern and southeastern regions of Mexico and in states with a large presence of 
indigenous peoples. T he states that make up the PA widely exceed the national average for poverty and extreme 
poverty : 4´935,00 people live in moderate and 2´131,800 people in extreme poverty conditions which represent 
20% of all Mexicans living in these conditions8. The highest levels of poverty by municipalities occurs in those 
with the largest indigenous population: in 8 out of 10 municipalities that have the highest poverty rate 97% or more of their population speaks an indigenous language.  
3. Mexico has been suffering from violence and insecurity, mainly due to the fight against organized crime 
contributing to a context of fragility in rural areas. Some micro- regions within the states of Morelos, Guerrero, 
Michoacán and Jalisco in the Balsas Basin are experiencing severe situations of social fragility and violence, and 
their homicide rates were above the national average in 2019 and 2020. For this reason, during the last years, 
the Government has transferred a more important role to the armed forces for the maintenance of public security. 
Violence is one of the common reasons why (young) people decide to migrate and is also a hindrance to achieve 
better women’s participation and gender transformation. Strengthening of the social fabric and the dy namization 
of sustainable and inclusive local economies are important contributions of this project to governmental efforts to confront violence and migration.  
 
Figure 1: Percentage of population living in poverty (dark blue > 51.1%): Guerrero 66.4%, Oaxaca 61.7%

 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 5 
 
 Source:  CONEVAL 2020  
 
4. The usual inequity faced by women to access different services and assets have been aggravated by the 
effects of climate change. On the one hand, the activities traditionally assigned for backyard orchard subsistence 
production ( traspatio), housework and family care, mostly managed by women, become more burdensome due 
to the increase in temperatures, water scarcity or water excess, in addition to the absence of public drinking water 
service. And on the other hand,  the use of non- forest timber products (palm, aromatic and medicinal plants, fruits, 
etc.), which are also often conducted by women, are affected by the loss of vegetation due to droughts, fires, or 
other extreme weather events, such as hurricanes and torr ential rains. Disasters, such as landslides and floods, 
affect houses and cause losses to fruit production, farm animals and domestic goods, situations that must be faced, mainly by the women in charge of their families. In addition to the economic losses,  the climatic events 
mentioned before usually contribute to illnesses and accidents, which also lead to additional burden for the 
women who usually take care of their relatives
9. In addition to the increasing household burdens women are more 
prone to gender -based violence in emergency and disaster contexts.  
5. While rainfed agriculture10 (mainly maize, beans, vegetable and fruit trees) and pastoralism (mainly cattle 
and goats) are the main sources of subsistence, forestry plays also a fundamental role in the livelihoods of the rural population of the PA. Of the total area of approximately 18 million hectares, 11.4 milli on hectares (62%) are 
covered with forest ecosystems that belong mainly to ejidos
11, indigenous and agrarian communities, all 
henceforth called Agrarian Communities (ACs)12. These territories offer a huge potential for forest landscape 
restoration and sustainable forest management including timber and non- timber forest products (NTFPs) for 
domestic and international markets. In 2021, 450 ACs obtained a permit from the Minist ry of Environment and 
Natural Resources (SEMARNAT) to harvest timber on an area of 375,000 ha and 54,500 ha for NTFPs. The latter 
include pine resin, olinalá  essence (Bursera linaloe) , oak charcoal, palm fiber (Brahea dulcis)  for production of 
handicrafts, aromatic and medicinal plants (including Damiana, Guachalalate, Oregano ) and wild agaves to 
produce syrup, fibers and artisanal mezcal.  
 
Figure 2: Land use in the Balsas and Pacific Coastal Plains Area  
 
Source:  Authors with data from INEGI, Serie VII 2018  
6. Forest and natural vegetation, which are crucial for the conservation of water flow regulation and 
biodiversity conservation have been under severe pressure in the PA: the main drivers for deforestation and 
forest degradation have been unsustainable agricu lture and extensive livestock activities accounting to 664,700 
and 904,700 ha between years 2002 and 2018, respectively.  
 
 
Figure 3: Land use changes in the Balsas Project Region, years 2002- 2018

7. Approximately 15.6 million young people (4 out of every 10) live in rural areas in Mexico. Of these youths, 
25.2% of young women and 7.6% of young men neither study or work and the proportion involved in agriculture 
has been falling. Not so in the PA were young people make up from 44% to 55% of the population. Rural youth 
represent more than 60% of the total population in Oaxaca and Guerrero. About 67% of these young people in 
Oaxaca and 36% in Guerrero are indigenous. 13 In small and isolated rural communities, youths aged 15- 19 may 
drop out of or leave school. These school dropouts may then work as day -laborers (1.5%) or in underpaid 
agricultural work and other similar activities (0.9%). Multidimensional poverty among rural youth (60%) is nearly twice as high as among urban youth (36%). A lack of opportunities for rural youth is linked to their difficulty in 
gaining access to the factors of production (including land rights and land ownership), labor markets, education, and financial resources. In the absence of economic opportunities, or the possibility of participating in community 
decision -making and governance systems, young people in rural areas are more likely to migrate than adults and 
are at a greater risk of being co-opted or victims of crime (organized, common crime, human trafficking).  
 8. In the Balsas Basin 1´891,985 people speak and indigenous language which represents more than a 
quarter (25,7%) of the total indigenous peoples in México 
14. Afro -descendent people make up more than 10% 
of the population of the states Guerrero and Oaxaca15. Thus, Oaxaca is the state with the highest percentage of 
indigenous people (43%) at the national level and in the project area: 16.3% of all indigenous people in the country live in Oaxaca. In Guerrero, there are 600,000 indigenous peoples who comprise 19.3% of the total state 
population. Historically, indigenous and afro- descendent peoples have the highest rates of poverty in Mexico. In 
2016, 72% (8.2 million) of indigenous people lived in poverty. The Human Development Index (HDI) for the 
indigenous population (0.6761) is lower than that
 for the non- indigenous population (0.7628) and shows a gap for 
multiple socioeconomic indicators when compared to national averages. The situation is even more adverse for 
indigenous women living in rural areas (8 out of 10 live in poverty and 4 out of 10 in extreme poverty).16 The 
Indigenous Peoples Plan for this Project is included in Annex 6b.  
 9. In Mexico only 30.9% of rural households have an adequate level of food security, 40.7% suffer mild food 
insecurity, 17.7% moderate insecurity and the remaining 11.2% high insecurity. States with higher levels of 
poverty also record the highest level of fo od shortages and are all located in the southern and southeastern 
regions of the country. Data from the pandemic period in 2020 show that the proportion of people suffering from 
severe food insecurity increased from 9.2% in 2018 to 13%.
17 The states with the highest levels of poverty also 
had the highest level of food shortages and are all located in the southern and southeastern regions of the country. 
Currently, 44% of households benefit from public food aid programs, which provide great er assistance to both 
indigenous and low -income households (70% of both receive assistance), as well as those suffering from 
moderate or severe food insecurity levels.18 The number of beneficiary households of some food aid programs is 
even higher in areas of the PA, for example reaching 65.8% of the population in the state of Guerrero.  
 
10. The ongoing health crisis resulting from COVID -19 has affected Mexico, exacerbating climate change 
vulnerabilities in the country. The United Nations Economic Commission for Latin America (ECLAC, 2020) 
estimates that the 9% observed drop in output linked to the pandemic will result in 9.5 and 7.9 million persons 
falling into poverty and extreme poverty, respectively.19 The Mexican National Council for the Evaluation of Social

 Development Policy (CONEVAL, 2020) also estimates 8.9 to 9.8 million new persons falling into poverty and 6.1 
to 10.7 million into extreme poverty due to the pandemic.20 
 
Over view of  climate trends affecting the Project Area  
 
Ecoregional climate analysis of the Project Area  
11. The Project Area is divided by two mountain ranges and includes two different lowlands. It can be divided 
in at least four ecoregions (Fig. 4) with different climate: a.) The Inter -montane Depressions (DI), inner lowlands 
characterized by low deciduous for est and xerophilous scrub vegetation, that cover an area of 7 million hectares; 
b.) The Southern Pacific Coastal Plains and hill landscape (PCP) with an area of 2.5 million hectares; c.) The 
temperate mountain ranges of the Sierra Madre del Sur (SMS) with an area of 5.7 million hectares and; d.) The 
Transverse Neovolcanic System (TNS) temperate mountain ranges of with nearly 3 million hectares.  
 
Figure 4: Four main ecoregions in the Project Area, and location of selected weather stations used for the 
analysis  
 
Source:  Authors ( see Annex 2b)  
12. To gain a more differentiated insight on the impact of climate change and climate variability on rural 
livelihoods and vulnerable ecosystems, daily climatological data from weather stations located in the Project Area 
were obtained from the Hydrological Information System (SIH) of the National Water Commission CONAGUA21. 
As year 2018 was the last year with sufficient data for the stations that had information since 1970, 2000- 2018 
was defined as the study period and 1970- 1999 as the reference period. Data from 1,251 stations were reviewed, 
of which 262 were selected with in the four ecoregions as these had complete data for both time periods. The 
distribution of these weather stations by eco- region within the PA is shown through red dots in the map in Figure 
4 above,  and its number by eco- region the Table 1 below:

13. Observed increase in precipitations  - Precipitation dynamics within the PA are predominantly influenced 
by the El Niño- Southern Oscillation (ENSO) System. During the study period (2010 -2018), the region experienced 
the "La Niña" phase of ENSO, leading to heightened rainfall levels across the area. Consequently, a marked 
increase in precipitation was observed across most ecoregions, ranging from +5.0% of precipitation in ID to 
+17.4% of precipitation in SMS. Over the same timeframe, there was a reduction in the frequency of dry days, 
with dec reases ranging from - 3 to - 7 days during the dry season and from - 2 to - 8 days during the wet season. 
Regarding extreme rainfall occurrences, the percentage of abnormally wet months (defined as months with a 
precipitation anomaly exceeding 50 mm) increased across most ecoregions of the PA, with increments ranging 
from +1.3% points in PCP to +7.5% points in SMS. Conversely, the percentage of abnormally dry months (months 
with a precipitation anomaly below - 50 mm) decreased, ranging from - 2.7% points in PCP t o -7.1% points in SMS. 
The only exception was observed in the ID, where the percentage of abnormally wet months remained stable ( -
0.5% points), while the percentage of abnormally dry months increased by +3.3% points.

14. Observed Increase in temperatures  - A notable rise in temperature was observed across the PA between 
the study and reference period. Maximum temperatures saw an increase ranging from +0.17°C in ID to +0.46°C 
in TNS, while minimum temperatures showed an increase from 0.50°C in TNS to +0.57°C in SMS. Here also, a 
notable exception must be mentioned in ID, where the minimum temperature decreased between the two period.  
 
Figure 6: Trends in annual maximum temperature in the 4 ecoregions  
 
 
Source:  Authors (see Annex 2b)  
15. Observed Increase in wet months occurrences  - The analysis of dry and wet month occurrences  in the 
PA was investigated using the Standardized Precipitation Evapotranspiration Index (SPEI)4. Across all 
ecoregions, there was a noticeable increase in the percentage of wet months, varying from 3.4% points in TNS 
to 12.7% points in SMS. Moreover, except for PCP, all ecoregions witnessed an increase in the proportion of wet 
 
4 SPEI is a comprehensive metric that integrates both precipitation and evapotranspiration data to assess the moisture conditions within a 
particular climate, relative to the average conditions for the corresponding month. Furthermore, SPEI incorporates information from 
preceding months to provide a holistic understanding of the current conditions within a broader temporal context. The SPEI can be used to assess the moisture or dryness levels of a given month, using thresholds as follow:
  
Wet month: SPEI > 1; Dry month:  SPEI < -1.

Severely wet month:  2> SPEI > 1.5;  Severely dry month:  -2 < SPEI < -1.5

16. Comparison with the regional context -  To provide regional context, we compared our results to the 
results based on the CRU TS observational dataset  obtained from the IPCC WGI interactive atlas, in Southern 
Central America, for the period 1980- 2015. Regional  data indicates an increase in precipitation of +0.06 mm/day 
per decade,  together with an increase of +0.15°C per decade.  These results confirmed the direction of the trends 
we found in the PA .

17. Projected Increase in temperatures  - A significant increase in temperatures is expected between the 
study period (1970- 1999) and the projection period (2041- 2060), across all ecoregions.  Under the mitigation 
scenario (SSP2- 4.5), an increase in maximum temperature ranging from +2.03°C in P CP to +2.43°C in SMS is 
expected, while for minimum temperature an increase ranging from +1.76°C in PCP to +3.18°C in ID is expected. 
Under the business -as-usual scenario, this increase becomes more pronounced. Maximum temperatures are 
projected to rise be tween +2.70°C in PCP to +3.60°C in ID, while minimum temperatures are expected to rise 
from +2.20°C in PCP to +4.00°C in ID.   
At the monthly scale, under the mitigation scenario, maximum temperature increases are expected to range between +2.5°C and +3.7°C, with the largest increases during the wet season, while minimum temperature 
increases are expected to range between +2.4°C and +3.9°C, with the largest increases during the dry season. 
Under the business -as-usual scenario maximum temperature increases are expected to be in the range of +3°C 
and +4.2°C, with the largest increases at the very end of the wet season, while minimum temperatures increases 
are expected to range between +3.07°C and +4.9°C, with the largest increases during the dry season.  
 
18. Divergent projected precipitation patterns  – Compared to the studied period, precipitation projections 
indicate two divergent paths depending on the scenario under consideration. Within the mitigation scenario 
(SSP2 -4.5), precipitation is expected to remain relatively stable, with minimal increases r anging from +0.1% in 
the ID to +0.5% in the TNS. An exception to this trend is however notable within the PA: SMS is projected to 
experience a significant - 14.2% decrease in precipitation under the mitigation scenario. Under the business -as-
usual scenario (SSP5 -8.5), a widespread decrease in precipitation is expected, ranging from - 1.9% in PCP to -
18.2% in SMS.  
 
19. Comparison with r egional context  - To provide regional context, we compared our results on climate 
projections to CORDEX downscaled data obtained from the IPCC WGI interactive atlas, for land in Southern 
Central America and the projection period of 2041- 2060, compared to the reference perio d of 1995- 2014. 
Regional  data indicates that the increase in maximum temperature is expected to be approximately +1.3°C under 
SSP2 -4.5 and +1.9°C under SSP5- 8.5. For minimum temperatures, regional  findings show an increase of +1.1°C 
under SSP2- 4.5 and +1.4°C under SSP5- 8.5. Regarding precipitation, regional data  anticipates a decrease of -
4.8% under SSP2- 4.5 and - 3.9% under SSP5- 8.5. These results underscore a notable contrast between our 
findings in the PA and the projected climate of the surrounding areas, with higher rates of increase in both maximum and minimum temperatures, and a more nuanced pattern in projected precipitation. 
It's however 
important to note that CORDEX data covers a larger geographical area and doesn't consider the specific


---
*Page 15*
---

B  
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 11 
 
 characteristics of each ecoregion. Moreover , it uses  a different  reference period  (1995 -2014 for CORDEX, 2000 -
2018 in our analysis) . Thus, this comparison should be approached with caution.  
 
20. Local s ensitivity to climate change exposure - Given that any long- term variation in temperature has a 
significant impact on crop production22, these results are of great relevance to the project area, where most of 
the rural population's livelihoods depend on agriculture. High temperatures alter enzyme function within a leaf 
and cause changes in the growth and development stages of the crop, leading to reduced yields. In the case of 
minimum temperature, several studies have addressed the effects of increased minimum temperature (night 
temperature) on crop physiology, which alters leaf enzyme function, decreases photosynthetic function, increases 
respiration rate, suppresses flower bud development, and accelerates crop maturity, which causes a decrease in 
yields23. Furthermore, the rates of temperature increase in tropical and sub- tropical areas are significantly higher 
in deforested areas, more than 50% higher than average temperature changes24. These changes will affect the 
suitability of the main crops that support rural livelihoods in the PA, especially livestock and coffee, increasing 
especially the vulnerability of poor families that depend on these for daily subsistence. The project will s upport 
vulnerable households to adapt to these higher temperatures supporting the transition from traditional cropping 
systems to silvopastoral and agroforestry systems with much more shade trees, crop species diversity and 
varieties better adapted to higher temperatures and drought. Moreover, the project will make efficient use of the 
ability of ecosystems and landscapes to buffer more frequent and more intense rainfall events and to store 
excessive rainwater masses through nature- based landscape restorati on and conservation solutions.  
 
Impact of drought  
21. According to the National Centre for the Prevention of Disasters CENAPRED25, during the period 2000-
2018, 1,337 emergency and disaster declarations at municipal level due to drought were registered in the PA. 
The municipalities that registered the highest number of declarations for these phenomena were in the DI and 
the PCP. Ninety -five per cent of these events were recorded betw een 2000 and 2009. The years with the highest 
number of declarations for this phenomenon were 2002, 2003, 2005 and 2009. In 2002, 311 declarations were recorded, most of which were issued at the expected beginning or during the rainy (planting) season and ended at the end of the rainy season. With respect to ENSO dynamics, during this period the transition from the neutral 
phase to El Niño was observed, which is associated with dry events during the rainy season in the PA. In 2003, 
135 emergency and disaster declarations were issued due to drought, which were effective in September and 
October (the second planting season). During this period, ENSO dynamics were in a neutral phase. In 2005, 269 drought emergency and disaster declarations were registered, 89% of which started in April and May, the months 
corresponding to the dry season. However, in most cases, their duration extended into September and even 
November. In 2005, ENSO dynamics were in transition from neutral to La Niña. In that sense, the combined 
effects of both phases can be associated with dry events during the rainy season (planting) and extend into the 
dry season at the end of the year. In 2009, 216 warnings were recorded, most of which were issued at the 
beginning of the rainy season and remai ned in effect until August and September. The ENSO dynamics for this 
period were in transition from La Niña to El Niño, so the dry events are attributed to this phenomenon.  
 
 
Figure 7: Declarations of emergencies and disasters due to droughts at municipal level in the Project Area

Source: CENAPRED, elaboration authors - Annex 2.b  
 
22. The climatic changes presented above will continue to have a direct impact on agriculture and forestry. 
Moreover, if no action is taken an expected 6.8% to 15.8% reduction in primary soil productivity could happen26 
due to prolonged dry season, more intense and erratic precipitation during the rainy season, and rising 
temperatures. Combined with all the economic and technological challenges to agriculture production in Mexico 
and, especially in PA, climate hazards have already imposed severe restrictions on farmers. For maize, Gómez 
et al. (2019) confirm that a lack of precipitation during July, combined with temperatures over 38°C, kills emerged 
seedlings. Along with this, drought for more than 21 days during August constituted a primary indicator of high 
climatic hazard to produce maize. In addition, this study evaluated 18 years (1999 to 2016) and concluded that 
the years with more than ten days with a temperature higher than 38°C matched with the years of low production 
and high climatic hazard. These findings are concerning when analyzing the observed changes in the PA, as also 
other similar findings reported27. 
 23. Ecosystems will also see other changes such as increase in plant diseases, and significant decline in 
mangrove areas and temperate forests as well as an increase in arid and semi -arid areas or dry forests. These 
changes will subsequently affect the provision of ecosystem services, especially water regulation and provision.
28  
 
Impacts of floods, landslides and atypical heavy rains  
24. During the period 2000- 2018, 870 municipal declarations of emergencies and disasters due to floods, 
flashfloods, landslides, and atypical rains were registered in the PA. The municipalities with the highest number 
of declarations for these phenomena were i n the PCP and SMS. Two thirds of these events were recorded 
between 2010 and 2018. The years with the highest number of declarations for this phenomenon were 2007, 
2010, 2014 and 2017. In 2007, 90 warnings were issued in three monthly periods: May (7), Jul y (13) and August 
(70). The ENSO dynamics during these periods, corresponding to the rainy season, correspond to La Niña, whose 
effects are observed in increased rainfall during the rainy season. In 2010, 144 emergency and disaster 
declarations were issued for floods and atypical rains in five monthly periods, of which two correspond to the dry 
season: January (29), February (75); and three to the rainy season: July (11), August (22) and September (7). 
During this period, ENSO dynamics were in a transition phase from El Niño to La Niña, characterized by intense 
precipitation events in the rainy season. In 2014, 119 warnings were issued in three monthly periods, most of 
them at the beginning of the rainy season: June (112), July (1) and September (6). The ENS O dynamics during 
these periods correspond to the neutral phase. In 2017, 167 warnings were issued in three periods: one during 
the dry season in March (4) and two during the rainy season, in June (42) and September (121). The ENSO 
dynamics for this period were in the La Niña phase, so rainfall in the rainy season was more intense.

Impact of tropical cyclones  
25. During the period 2000- 2018, 2,090 municipal declarations of emergencies and disasters due to tropical 
cyclones were registered in the PA. The municipalities that registered the highest number of declarations for 
these phenomena were in the PCP and SMS. Eighty -two per cent of the declarations were registered in five 
years: 2000, 2005, 2012, 2013 and 2014. In 2000, 760 emergency declaratio ns were issued, the highest number 
recorded during the study period. This atypical number of declarations can be associated with the occurrence of 
two tropical storms (Norman and Rosa) that made landfall in the areas of the PA where the municipalities with  
the highest risk of tropical cyclones are located29. During these months, ENSO dynamics were in the La Niña 
phase.  
 
Figure 9: Declarations of emergencies and disasters due to tropical cyclones in the Project Area  
 
Source: CENAPRED, elaboration authors - Annex 2.b  
26. More than half of the municipalities in the PA have some level of vulnerability to climate change.  In the 
case of hazards and risks due to hydrometeorological events, these do show patterns associated with the 
ecoregions. The municipalities located in the PCP and DI have a higher level of danger due to drought and floods, 
while those located in the PCP and SMS have a higher level of risk from tropical cyclones than the rest of the ecoregions. The most notorious patterns of the effects of climate change and variability can be seen in disaster 
declarations due to droughts and floods. In the case of drought declarations, these were most frequent between 
2000 and 2009 during the ENSO El Niño phase. However, between 2010 and 2018, La Niña and the neutral 
phase predominated, resulting in fewer drought declarations, but more declarations for floods and atypical rains. 
In the case of tropical cyclone declarations, there is an association between the months in which the declarations 
were issued and the presence of La Niña and ENSO neutral phase.  020406080100120140160180Number of declarationsDeclarations of emergencies and disasters due to flood 
and atypical rainfall at municipal level

 27. In terms of water resources, climate change will exacerbate shortages that are already being experienced 
by rural ACs in the PA, both at the household level (drinking water) and the on- farm level (water for irrigation). 
The impacts of climate change on the water cycle will not be uniform, revealing significant vulnerability and risk 
exposure to certain ecosystems and many human activities (domestic and agricultural) given climate variability. 
For freshwater, renewable surface and groundwater is expected to decline during the 21st century, especially in 
dry subtropical regions, increasing competition among users and reducing supplies to ACs. In addition, the effects 
of climate change will be accentuated in areas with rapid urbanization processes . The impacts will also be felt in 
rural areas due to the reduced availability of water and changes in temperature, c ausing displacement of 
agricultural areas and, consequently, a ffecting both the rural population and food security in general.  
 Combined economic impacts  
28. The hydrometeorological events described above have resulted in both the loss of human life and 
enormous financial impacts. Recent adverse climatic events have affected 2.4 million people in the region and 
caused 561 deaths. These events also damaged 147,000 homes, 1.4 million ha of cropland and 14,000 km of 
roads. The concurrent economic losses have been estimated at USD 4,279 million.  All these factors, thus, 
increase the vulnerability of the Basin’s populations and ecosystems. In fact, the Balsas Basin is one of the two 
main areas in Mexico with the highest rates of climatic vulnerability. 68.3% of the Basin (12.2 million ha) 
experiences average vulnerability, 27.4% (4.9 million ha) high vulnerability and only 4.3% (0.78 million ha) low vulnerability.
30 The interaction between social, economic, and environmental vulnerability in the Basin converges 
in Guerrero and Oaxaca, being the most affected states in southern Mexico. Most (97.7%) of the Basin’s natural 
and productive centers with medium to high climate vulnerability are affected by water issues, especially problems 
linked to water distribution. 89.6% of agricultural areas are also affected by soil -related vulnerabilities (particularly 
inadequate soils for growing rainy season crops). 88% of forest ar eas are vulnerable in terms of their supporting 
capacity or for having ecological niches with representative species. 78% of livestock areas are vulnerable in 
terms of their naturally available animal food supplies. Finally, 62% of biodiversity areas are vulnerable due to the impacts of climate change on their plant c ommunities.
31  
 
 
Mitigation rationale: GHG emissions profile  
29. According to Mexico's Third Biannual Report to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate 
Change32 and to Mexico´s Greenhouse Gas Inventory 1990- 201933 in 2019 the total estimated net emissions in 
all sectors at the national level were 534,688.6 MtCO2e. The “Agriculture, Forestry and Other land Uses” sector contributed 19.12%, second after the Energy sector (63.52%). In contrast reforestation and preserv ing forests, 
farmland, or grasslands helped absorb CO ₂ from the atmosphere to release oxygen and generate biomass to 
store 201.94 million tons of CO ₂ in the year 2019.  
30. For the specific case of the Project Area and focusing on the forestry sector, gross emissions during the 
period 2001- 2018 were estimated at 76.1 MtCO
2eq34. Of these emissions, conversion from forest to grazing land 
accounted for 72.2% (54.9 MtCO 2eq); 26.3% corresponded from forest conversion to agricultural land (20.1 
MtCO 2eq) and the remaining 1.1% of emissions came from conversion from forests to human settlements and 
other types of land use. Emissions linked to the expanding agricultural frontier in the Basin are occurring in a context in which most or 65% (11.7 million ha) of the total land area is held by ACs.
35  
 
Situation of deforestation and FREL in the Balsas Basin 
31. In line with the definitions used in the Second FREL for the period 2007- 2016 submitted by Mexico in 
2020,36 an assessment of the annual forest loss in the Balsas Basin was undertaken through 9,200 plots within 
the grid of the National Forest Inventory (INFyS, as to its acronym in Spanish). While the gross annual 
deforestation for the whole period between the years 2001- 2012 was of around 32,675 ha / year, annual 
deforestation increased by 16% for the period 2013- 2018 to reach 37,856 ha / year. The total forest area lost 
between years 2001 and 2018 was 616,595 ha, of which 63.8% corresponded to dry forests and 36.2% to 
temperate montane forests. More than 70% of these forests were lost to pastureland and the remainder mainly 
for other agricultural uses during that period.37 Additional details on deforestation in the Balsas Basin are 
presented in Annex 2. It is to be noted that the Second FREL for the period 2007 -2016 submitted by Mexico in 
2020 includes two of the five REDD+ activities: reduction of emissions from deforestat ion, and partial reduction 
of emissions from forest degradation.  
 
 
Alignment with the NDCs, the ENAREDD+, and other national priorities  
32. The measures set out in Mexico's Nationally Determined Contributions38 (NDCs) respond to mitigation 
and adaptation problematics in the Project Area. According to the NDCs, mitigation activity in the land use, land


---
*Page 19*
---

B  
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 15 
 
 use change and forestry sector ( uso del suelo, cambio de uso del suelo y silvicultura  USCUSS) directly aligns 
with the overarching aim of ecosystem- based adaptation. The NDCs recognize the need to incorporate climate 
criteria into forestry and rural development programs. It also recognizes  the need to implement environmental 
protection and land use standards. For this reason, the project framework aims to contribute to the NDCs 
mitigation and adaptation goals indicated in axes A, B, C and D.39 Contributions will occur firstly in terms of (i) 
Climate Change Adaptation. This will involve activities focusing on both (a) social sector adaptation to climate 
change, (b) ecosystem- based adaptation and (c) water resource and supply management. Contributions will 
occur secondly in terms of (ii) Climate Change Mitigation. This will involve reducing emissions through activities focusing on (a) the land use sector via land use change and forestry activit ies (e.g., afforestation, reforestation, 
forest management, farmland management and pasture management) and (b) the agricultural sector via 
improved agricultural soil management, scheduled soil burns, improved silvopastoral practices, rotational grazing and manure management. A detailed matrix that shows the alignment of project outputs and activities with each 
of the axis and lines of action of Mexico´s NDCs is presented in Annex 2a, Chapter 5.  
33. The project will support the implementation of Mexico’s National Strategy for Reducing Emissions from 
Deforestation and Forest Degradation 2017- 2030 ( Estrategia Nacional de Reducción de Emisiones por 
Deforestación y Degradación Forestal  ENAREDD+)40. This strategy aims to achieve the following milestones by 
2030:  
● Achieve 0% net deforestation rate by 2030.  
● Significant reduction in the national rate of forest degradation compared to the baseline.  
● Achieve an increase in sustainably managed forest area, natural and induced regeneration and forest  
conservation, with a consequent increase in carbon stocks.  
● Maintain or improve the provision of environmental services of the territory by conserving biodiversity  
● The continued development of the social and economic capital of rural communities.  
● Increase the use of best practices of sustainable production at the rural landscape scale.  
34. The Balsas Project will support the achievement of all the above mentioned six milestones of the 
ENAREDD+. Furthermore, the Project will implement most of the activities of the seven components and 
corresponding lines of action of the ENAREDD+ in the Project Area, as shown in the alignment matrix included 
in Annex 2a, Chapter 5.  
35. The National Forest Programme (PNF) 2020– 202441 is a Special Programme derived from the National 
Development Plan 2019 –2024, led by CONAFOR, with the participation of forest sector stakeholders and the 
approval of the National Forest Council. PNF’s purpose is to contribute to the fulfillment  of international 
commitments relating to forests that Mexico has undertaken by entering into a series of multilateral agreements. 
The PNF covers a total of 26 strategies and 188 specific actions, including the National Strategy for Community 
Forest Management, the ENAREDD+, the Fire Management Programme,  the Programme to curb Illegal Logging 
and Illegal Trade in raw materials and forest products, the Strategy to promote active conservation through 
Payment for Environmental Services, the Strategy for Productive Restoration with an integrated land 
management approach, the Strategy for a Regulatory and Administrative Framework to facilitate Sustainable 
forest development, and the Strategy for Neighborhood Territorial Management, among others.  
36. The PFN includes five priority objectives: 1.) Promote community forest management for the sustainable 
and diversified use of forest resources, as well as the integration and development of local networks of 
competitive value that trigger local economies t o improve the quality of life of the population living in forest areas, 
in particular women, youth and indigenous peoples. 2.) Protect Forest ecosystems from factors that lead to 
deterioration in the vegetation cover to maintain the natural heritage and contribute to climate change mitigation, 
for the well -being of the population living in forest areas and of society in general, through territorial management. 
3.) Conserve and restore the capacity to provide ecosystem services in strategic forest areas, thr ough an inclusive 
and participatory approach that contributes to guaranteeing a healthy environment for the development and well -
being of the population. 4.) Promote a new model of governance, plurality and effective and inclusive social and public partici pation in the forestry sector. 5. – Promote effective institutional coordination and improve institutional 
capacity -building for the forestry sector.  
37. The BALSAS project will implement the mandate of the General Law for the Sustainable Forest 
Development and will be integrated within CONAFOR´s “ Programa de Apoyos para el Desarrollo Forestal 
Sustentable para el Bienestar ” (PADFSB). This program supports Agrarian Communities to implement actions 
that contribute to the protection, conservation, restoration and incorporation into sustainable forest management of natural forests and forest land. The program also strengthens value chai ns, which in turn contribute to climate 
change adaptation and mitigation. It is made up of five PADFSB  Incentive Schemes
: I. Community Forest 
Management and Value Chains (MFCCV); II. Commercial Forest Plantations and Agroforestry Systems (PFC A); 
III. Forest Restoration of Micro- basins and Strategic Regions (RFM); IV. Environmental Services ; (PSA)  and V. 
Forest Protection (PF). All outcomes s, in a cross- cutting way, will target women, youth and indigenous peoples, 
and propose specific activities for their empowerment (see Annex 8). Its approved budget for year 2021 was

---

 825.5 million MXP. Additionally, compensation payments for authorized forest land use changes (CAxCUSF) that 
accrue into the Mexican Forestry Fund (Fondo Forestal Mexicano)  will also co -finance project activities.  
 
Link to other ongoing initiatives and projects in the project area  
38. Early actions of REDD+, that started in 2012 with the support of the Forest and Climate Change project 
(World Bank/CONAFOR), were focused on the Yucatan Peninsula and coastal area of Jalisco. Other relevant 
initiatives are the Emission Reduction Initiative supported by the FCPF (2016)42. The document  Mapeo del 
Financiamiento para- REDD+ en México43 provides  an overview of all REDD+ financing.  Although these initiatives 
were not located in the Balsas basin CONAFOR’s institutional capacit ies were strengthened, particular ly 
environmental and social safeguard management , Payment for Environmental Services (PES) modalities and 
financial tools to strengthen AC inclusion in forestry value chains, on which this proposal is building upon.  
39. The GEF co-financed GreenMex project (USD 10 .1 million  GEF Grant / 2022- 2027) will mainstream 
biodiversity conservation, integrated landscape management and ecosystem connectivity into CONAFOR´s 
PADFSB . The project will be implemented in forest and agroforestry landscapes within three areas of high 
biodiversity in Mexico, one of them the Balsas / South Pacific Region. GreenMex will have a synergistic effect 
with the BALSAS project that seeks to integrate climate adaptation criteria into the PADFSB  and its institutional 
setting.  CONAFOR as Executing Agency is being supported by  FAO as GEF Implementing Agency  and FAO will 
be invited to the Balsas Project Steering Committee CDAL,  since  one of the three Mexican regions covered by 
GreenMex  is this same project area of  the BALSAS Project.  GreenMex will be financing nature- based solutions 
for biodiversity and landscape management to strengthen biological connectivity eight in bio-forestry corridors  on 
an area of 2 ´712,633 ha in 116 municipalities in the Balsas Basin in the States of Michoacán, Guerrero and 
Oaxaca, in strong alignment and complementarity with the Balsas Project. A t the institutional and project 
coordination level synergies will be  sought  between both projects . 
40. Another initiative on which this project will build upon is the “Strengthening Entrepreneurship in 
Productive Forest Landscapes Project ” (PROFOEM44 - World Bank/CONAFOR , USD 66M loan, closing 09/2024) 
which has been piloting new schemes of PES and sustainable forest management. The new PES approach 
combines  restoration and forest ecosystem management with PES  through PADFSB program RFM.1 : 
“Restauración forestal de microcuencas estratégicas ”. It supports ACs to restore ecosystems over several years 
ensuring a long- term commitment to improving soil and vegetation management if progress is verified in the field. 
At least  60% of the transferred funds to ACs must be used for  forest ecosystem management and 40% can be  
between members of ACs. These advancements promote income generating activities  for women, as they are 
not dependent on land ownership, as was the case with previous PES approaches.  
41. Finally, the project "Connecting watershed health with sustainable livestock and agroforestry production" 
(CONECTA) aims to improve integrated landscape management and promote climate- smart production practices 
in selected watersheds, ensuring the social i nclusion by targeting women, youth and indigenous peoples. Its 
implementation period is five years from July 2021 to July 2026. It is managed by the Fondo Mexicano para la 
Conservación de la Naturaleza, A.C. (FMCN), which is responsible for the fiduciary aspects, and the Instituto 
Nacional de Ecología y Cambio Climático  (INECC), which is responsible for the technical leadership. In addition, 
nine federal agencies participate in the project through the Coordinating Committee, which seeks to scale 
CONECTA's impact through the alignment of both public and private investment s. CONECTA will concentrate its 
interventions in 15 eligible watersheds in the states of Chiapas, Chihuahua, Jalisco and Veracruz, all outside of 
the Balsas PA. The watershed approach responds to integrated landscape management, which will be driven by the development of Integrated Watershed Management Action Plans (PAMIC) led by INECC. Three Regional 
Funds (RF) will support local coordination and implementation of investments. The Balsas Project team will seek 
to learn from experiences and good practices and to cross -fertilize with this project.

diagram)  
Project Theory of Change S tatement  
42. IF bottom- up participatory mechanisms for intersectoral coordination,  and capacities to include climate 
change criteria in institutional structures and procedures , and participation in value chains are strengthened;  and 
inclusive, participatory, multi -annual land use and investment plans within prioritized areas are implemented to 
strengthen the productive systems of ACs; and climate hazard early warning systems and knowledge are in place 
THEN climate resilient production systems and sustainable land use forms will improve the livelihood resilience 
of the most vulnerable rural households and communities, and ecosystems and ecosystem services will be 
conserved in the project area and the populati on of the Balsas Basin will be better informed about climate hazards 
BECAUSE  government programs and community development plans integrate climate change criteria; forest 
landscapes are sustainably managed and better adapted to climate change and the population of the Balsas 
Basin has timely and relevant climate information for climate risk management, and disaster prevention.

 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 17 
 
 43. The core medium - to long -term result of the project is related to the application of paradigm shifting 
innovations, first piloted in the municipalities prioritized through the Balsas project using GCF concessional 
finance to be scaled- up to the regional an d national level. The average of the last five years of forest development 
finance provided by CONAFOR´s PADFSB  at the national level is equivalent to around USD 160 million per year. 
During the project lifespan of 20 years these would add up to a total of more than USD 1.54 billion (present value) 
of traditional forest development finance that will be transformed into sustainable forest development finance for 
climate change adaptation and mitigation in benefit of Mexico’s most vulnerable communities and within these communities, target women, youth and indigenous peoples. There is immense potential  for this leveraged finance 
to be increased further through the application of the same paradigm shifting innovations to other rural 
development finance sources in Mexico, such as CONAGUA´s PROAGUA Program, through the strengthening of multisectoral coordi nation structures via the project.  
44. This cross- cutting project (80% adaptation, 20% mitigation) aims to increase the climate resilience of the 
most vulnerable communities and ecosystems, and reduce emissions from production systems in the Balsas PA, 
via: i) operationalizing the use of climat e change criteria in current institutional modalities for the prioritization of 
rural development finance through a community -centric bottom- up approach; ii) investing in the climate- sensitive 
management of ecosystems, and climate- resilient productive systems and value chains for adaptation and 
mitigation benefits; and iii) developing a climate information sys tem, and implementation of knowledge 
management.  
 
45. The objective of the project is to enable community -level investment in adaptation and mitigation 
initiatives, through climate- focused transformation of rural development incentives for the conservation of forest 
resources, and the strengthening of local institutions and capacities . This objective will be achieved by the 
project’s strong focus on bottom- up decision- making and value chain development  ensuring social inclusion, 
benefiting and empowering women, youth and indigenous peoples . The proposed project will shift the approach 
of the current rural development support allocation system used by rural development institutions to a new, 
transformative approach which:  
● Uses climate change- related criteria in a participatory transparent way for selecting interventions and 
prioritizing the most vulnerable beneficiaries, and sites.  
● Strengthens territorial governance and participation of women, youth, and indigenous peoples at the 
sub-basin level to provide incentives and strengthen inter institutional coordination, fostering a 
bottom- up approach for the sustainable use,  management , and conservation of crucial ecosystems 
and ecosystem services to be piloted in the prioritized municipalities of the Balsas PA and replicated 
at regional and national level. As part of the project’s bottom- up approach, the participation of 
Agrarian Commun ities and Producer Organizations in value chains of agroforestry, timber and non-
timber forest products (NTFPs) will also be strengthened, with the aim of increasing the incomes of 
the most vulnerable households, thereby building their adaptive capacity.  
● Disburses rural incentives multi -annually under a progress -based payment system, specifically 
rewarding performance of adaptation and mitigation initiatives. The multi -annual approach and 
annual evaluation of progress was first piloted by CONAFOR for PES s chemes only and will be 
replicated by the project across other forest development support schemes under CONAFOR´s 
PADFSB . 
 
Outcomes  
The three outcomes presented below contribute collectively to the achievement of the project’s objective. These will be achieved via a results chain between activities and outputs as described below.  
 
46. Outcome 1: Government programs and community development plans integrate climate change 
criteria for allocation of supports . This outcome will be achieved through:  
● Strengthening mechanisms for intersectoral coordination, which will build the technical and 
organizational capacities of small producers and facilitate synergies between government institutions 
and the private sector (Output 1.1). This will include creating Forest Management Units (FMU) and the 
corresponding Regional Committees for the Integrated Management of the Territory (RCIMT) at basin, 
sub-basin and ethnic territory levels (Activity 1.1.1), and strengthening multi -sectoral coordination 
structures, operation rules and mechanisms for co- investment in basins (Activity 1.1.3). Additionally, 
priority intervention areas for climate change adaptation will be identified (Activity 1.1.2), while criteria 
for the selection of appropriate adaptation interventions will be integrated into relevant institutional 
territorial development procedures  (Activity 1.1.4).  
● Developing participatory climate- responsive (adaptation and mitigation) multiannual investment plans 
for prioritized areas (Output 1.2). This will include raising  awareness amongst agrarian and indigenous


---
*Page 22*
---

B  
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 18 
 
 communities on how to benefit from incentive programs that promote climate resilience (Activity 1.2.1) 
followed by the development of investment plans at the sub- basin level (Activity 1.2.2).  
● Building the capacities of local actors (including extension workers, local facilitators, and forest 
protection brigades  (Activity 1.3.1) and CONAFOR’s local extension offices, “ Promotorías Forestales ” 
(Activity 1.3.3) to plan and implement inclusive adaptation approaches that enhance resilience (Output 1.3). This will include supporting the establishment and operation of forest protection brigades and 
climate change resilience facilitators in the project’s target municipalities (Activity 1.3.2).  
 
47. Outcome 2: Forest landscapes are sustainably managed and better adapted to climate change. 
This outcome will be reached through:  
● Conserving, restoring and sustainably using forest landscapes by agrarian communities through the 
implementation of land- use plans and PES mechanisms (Activity 2.1.1; Output 2.1). Enhanced capacity 
and governance achieved via Outcome 1 will provide a platf orm to support the achievement of Output 
2.1. 
● Strengthening the climate resilience of agrarian communities’ productive systems (Output 2.2), through 
the implementation of climate- smart forest landscape restoration in micro- watershed and strategic 
areas (Activity 2.2.1) and climate adapted commercial f orest plantations and agroforestry systems 
(Activity 2.2.2).  
● Enhancing the participation of agrarian communities and producer organizations, especially women 
and the youth, in forestry and agroforestry value chains and building the climate resilience of their 
livelihoods (Output 2.3). This will include building loca l capacity for and implementing climate- resilient 
sustainable forest management and ensuring inclusive access to timber and non -timber forest product 
value chains (Activity 2.3.1), supporting households to improve food and nutrition security (Activity 2.3.2), and establishing community forest protection brigades to monitor and ensure the sustainable 
climate -responsive use of forest resources (Activity 2.3.3).  
 
48. Outcome 3: The producers, inhabitants and authorities of the Balsas PA have timely and relevant 
climate information for climate risk management, disaster prevention. This outcome will be achieved 
through:  
● Supporting accessible climate hazard- related early warning and monitoring systems in the Balsas PA 
(Output 3.1) by developing a platform and applications to facilitate access to climate information and 
early warning systems (Activity 3.1.1), strengthening following of CONAFOR’s crucial systems: i.) The 
Forest Fire Protection and Prevention System (SPPIF), ii.) The Forest Phytosanitary Surveillance 
(SIVICOFF) System (Activity 3.1.2), iii.) The Project -specific Monitoring, Reporting and Verification 
(MRV) System (Activity 3.1.3), and iv.) The Social and Environmental Safeguards Management System 
(Activity 3.1.4).  
● Developing policy -relevant knowledge products through systematic documentation of good practices 
and intervention approaches of the project (Output 3.2), including the production of training and 
systemization materials to support ongoing capacity developme nt (Activity 3.2.1), exchange of 
experiences, and South- South and Triangular Cooperation (SSTC) at the local, regional, national and 
international levels (Activity 3.2.2), and rolling out and operating a rigorous monitoring and evaluation 
(M&E) system for the project (Activity 3.2.3).  
 
49. Barriers:  The proposed project has been designed to respond to specific barriers to the achievement of 
its proposed outcomes and project objective. These barriers and the activities designed to address them are 
presented below.  
50. Barrier set 1.  A key factor shaping climate- related challenges is the weak level of existing climate 
governance for the design and implementation of climate change adaptation and mitigation initiatives, as well as 
the allocation of climate finance, since efforts to integrate a climate approach into public policies have only 
recently begun. Furthermore, institutional coordination among the involved actors, specialized technical 
assistance on climate change and involvement of community perspectives and participation have been limited. 
Specifically, the lack of technical assistance and coordination among actors, has delayed the roll out of climate 
change adaptation interventions across the Basin, because many of the actors are still used to business -as-usual 
development initiatives. Climate change adaptation interventions have therefore only been considered as an afterthought or co- benefit. As a result, there have been no clearly focused incentives encouraging concerted 
action for climate change mitigation and adaptation. These findings are supported by a recent independent


---
*Page 23*
---

B  
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 19 
 
 evaluation of CONAFOR’s ongoing work45 in the Balsas Basin which indicate s a sectorized vision of the territory, 
generating a contradiction between initiatives, as well as scattered and disorganized efforts, deficient coordination 
between central offices and the local Promotorías  in the design, definition, and implementation of the PADFSB . 
Regarding specialized technical assistance, the findings indicate little relationship in the development of support 
activities between the technical adviser and the beneficiaries, and shortcomings  of technical advisers. Lastly, 
regarding community participation and the participation of women and youth, the reports indicate a negative and 
low perception of ownership of the planning, monitoring, and evaluation processes, and weak governance in ACs. 
A coordinated approach will ensure that climate change adaptation is front and center  of development in the 
Basin, allowing for more effective budgeting and planning, and ultimately a greater impact on the climate 
resilience of local communities. These barriers will be addressed and overcome through the activities described below:  
● Facilitate the creation of Forest Management Units (FMUs) and the corresponding Regional Committees 
for the Integrated Management of the Territory (RCIMT), the statutory natural resource and watershed 
management unit and bodies in each Basin, Sub- basin and ethnic territories priorit ized by the Project 
(Activity 1.1.1)  and agree on priority intervention areas (Activity  1.1.2).  
● Strengthening multi -sectoral coordination structures, operation rules and mechanisms for co- investment 
in the prioritized basins (Activity 1.1.3).  
● Raise the awareness amongst agrarian and indigenous communities on how to benefit from incentive 
programs that promote climate resilience (Activity 1.2.1).  
● Developing participatory climate- responsive (adaptation and mitigation) multiannual investment plans at 
the sub- basin level with active involvement of women and youth (Activity 1.2.2).  
● Developing the capacities of local actors (including extension workers, local facilitators, and forest protection brigades (Activity 1.3.1) and of CONAFOR’s Promotorías Forestales  (Activity 1.3.3) to plan 
and implement inclusive adaptation approaches that enhance resilience, including supporting the 
establishment and operation of forest protection brigades and climate change resilience facilitators in the 
project’s target municipal ities (Activity 1.3.2).  
51. Barrier set 2.  The main institutions working in the Balsas PA (CONAFOR, CONAGUA, and 
AGRICULTURA ) have been developing advanced information systems related to weather forecast, forest fires, 
medium/long term evolution of climate patterns and climate risk prevention. However, climate information is 
hosted in different systems and platforms that are not interconnected, and therefore is not easily accessible by 
communities, municipalities and even extension services. The  local population cannot access this information as 
they rely on cellphones for information sharing, while climate information is normally only available through 
computers, which many community members do not have access to. Such information remains mainly  used by 
research and national institutes (e.g., Instituto Nacional de Investigaciones Forestales, Agrícolas y Pecuarias  - 
INIFAP), but its accessibility by ACs, municipalities and local technicians remains difficult. Consequently, climate 
risks and impacts cannot be effectively integrated into local planning processes, with appropriate consideration 
of local priorities, co ntext and knowledge. Evidence of this barrier is presented in several recent CONEVAL 
assessments (2019, 2021 and 2022)46. This includes: i) information systems that do not inform integral territorial 
development support budgets and disbursements to vulnerable communities; ii) scarce guidance on the operation of the Information Systems which prevents information and data fro
m being captured and used; iii) scarce 
information on climate change and the sustainable management of forest resources; iv) limited dissemination 
and use of the information generated by the National Monitoring, Reporting and Verification (MRV) System; v) a 
platform for registering PADFSB support requests online that is still in its infancy; and vi) little relation between 
the areas of forestry information, climate change, information technology, information, and operational areas to 
improve the efficiency of the systems. These barriers will be addressed through the activities described below:  
● Developing a platform and applications to facilitate access to climate information and early warning systems (Activity 3.1.1).  
● Strengthening CONAFOR’s Forest Fire Protection and Prevention System (SPPIF), Forest Phytosanitary 
Surveillance System (SIVICOFF) (Activity 3.1.2), the Project -specific Monitoring, Reporting and 
Verification System (MRV) (Activity 3.1.3), and the S ocial and Environmental Safeguards Management 
System (Activity 3.1.4) .  
52. Barrier set 3.  The restrictive impact  of Barrier sets 1 and 2 on a coordinated and informed approach to 
climate change adaptation and mitigation across the Balsas Basin is further exacerbated by the lack of finance 
available to local institutions and communities for local -level initiatives. C urrently, development finance is not 
informed by climate change criteria and therefore climate finance flows supporting the planning and 
implementation of climate change adaptation and mitigation initiatives in the Basin’s most vulnerable communities 
are largely absent. This barrier will be addressed through following project activities : 
● Communication and awareness -raising campaign to disseminate calls for proposals (Activity  1.2.1).


---
*Page 24*
---

B  
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 20 
 
 ● Strengthening participatory climate -responsive multiannual financial investment plans at the sub -basin 
level (Activity 1.2.2).  
● Forest landscape conservation will be supported through PES financed  through the PADS, as well as 
through the Local PES Mechanism (Activity 2.1.1)  
 
53. Assumptions  and Co -Benefits : The assumptions underlying the achievement of the project’s outcomes 
are described briefly hereafter: Assuming active and consistent support from all levels of institutional and local 
stakeholders, the uptake and implementation of climate -resilient forest landscape management in the Balsas PA 
will be achieved in a coordinated and sustainable manner. This wi ll be anchored on the willingness of vulnerable 
agrarian communities (including local indigenous peoples) and groups (including women and youth) to take up 
new and alternative approaches to the management of productive systems and forest ecosystems that increase 
productivity, improve value chains and marked access, and adaptive capacity under conditions of climate change and reduce the use of unsus tainable environmentally degradative practices. The uptake of such approaches will 
be fostered through an improved understanding of the use of climate and early warning information for decision making.  The main co- benefits , which are described further below in more detail in Section D.3 are the following: 
i.) Environmental services maintained,  ii.) Biodiversity conserved.

B.3. Project/programme  description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)  
54. Project Area Focalization:  To specifically target the highest levels of social and environmental 
vulnerability  in the Balsas Basin, the following targeting criteria were applied:  
● Vulnerability to climate change: Municipalities with high and very high vulnerability to climate change 
according to seven vulnerability dimensions established by the SEMARNAT47 and the National 
Institute of Ecology and Climate Change INECC48. 
● Poverty and social marginalization and prioritized indigenous territories : The level of poverty and 
social marginalization is being rated by the  National Council for the Evaluation of Social Policies 
CONEVAL . 
55. As to these focalization  criteria  CONAFOR and CONAGUA identified 104 municipalities49 with medium 
to high vulnerability and with high and very high poverty rates that are located in 8 watersheds . These watersheds  
have a high potential for forest landscape restoration and are important for the provision of environmental 
services, particularly soil protection for water flow regulation.  The prioritized watersheds are:  Río Mezcala- Balsas, 
Río Tlapaneco, Río Atoyac, Río Ometepec o Grande, Río Nexpa, Río Papagayo; and the Sub- watersheds Río 
la Arena and Río Mixteco. Additionally, 17 municipalities located in two indigenous territories, the Xoxo Mixteca  
(Puebla) and the Meseta Purépecha (Michoacán) were included, resulting in a total of 121 municipalities with the 
highest level of prioritization (orange color in Figure 11 below). Once piloted and successfully implemented in 
these municipalities, the project offers the opportunity for up scaling to 280 additional municipalities of high priority 
(yellow color, below) for a total of 401 municipalities in the Balsas Basin . The detailed analysis and the focalization 
strategy, as also the detailed list of the municipalities can be found in Annex 2a.

the watersheds).

56. The project aims to increase capacity for resilience, adaptation and climate risk management for 
approximately 211,633 people, women, children and men from vulnerable rural households, assuring social 
inclusion of women, youth and including indigenous communities. They represent 9 .25% of the population of  the 
10 prioritized areas (8 basins and sub- basins and 2 indigenous territories).  Among this target population, the 
project will directly benefit 40% women5, 58% indigenous peoples ( Nahua, Mixteco, Tlapaneco, Amuzgo, among 
others) and at least 30% youths. The project will also include other vulnerable groups, mainly the Afro- Mexican 
population. Detailed project beneficiary calculations are presented in Annex 23. The project is structured into 
three interrelated, complementary, and mutually reinforcing outcomes as described below.  
 
Outcome  1: Government programs and community development plans integrate climate change criteria 
for allocation of support (USD 10´091 726) 
 
 
5 Recalling the national context in which women with ejido or communal ownership rights represent about 23%, 
and that CONAFOR''s programs are essentially aimed at those who have rights over the land and associated natural resources. Through a new special sub- program for settled women (see rules of operation), CONAFOR 
seeks to increase benefits to this sector, but its ability to submit proposals for such support is limited. The latest 
figures from CONAFOR in the communities supported by the PROFOEM project (World Bank), showed that the 
percentage of women beneficiaries was around 26% (this would be the baseline).  In other words, traditionally 
the participation of women is much lower than 40%. This has been also confirmed during consultation activities 
during preparation. Hence, the project proposed an ambitious, yet realistic target of 40% in the project, which it 
thrives to achieve through its intense gender participation approach.
