 Output 1.1: Mechanisms for intersectoral coordination are strengthened to include climate change 
criteria in institutional structures and procedures . 
57. Activity 1.1.1. Facilitate the creation of Forest Management Units (FMUs) and Regional Committees for 
the Integrated Management of the Territory (RCIMTs).  
The General Law of Sustainable Forest Development50 mandates that CONAFOR delimits Forest Management 
Units (FMUs), based on physical, environmental, social, and economic aspects, with the purpose of managing 
forest landscapes sustainably, securing orderly planning of activities and an efficient management of the natural 
resources. As FMUs in the Balsas have  yet to be created the Project will support the establishment of FMUs in 
the project area in the prioritized basins, and sub- basins, and the two identified indigenous territories. The project 
will also facilitate the development of Regional Committees for the Integrated Management of the Territory 
(RCIMTs). These innovative representation schemes will allow for meaningful participation of agrarian 
communities, indigenous communities, producer organizations, women, and youth, in addition to CONAFOR, local representatives, institutions of the states, federal entities and the private sector.  
58. At the level of each state, CONAFOR has created consultation bodies “Consejos Forestales Estatales”  
(CFE), to provide guidance and advice on forest management programs and the development of value chains for 
timber and non- timber forest products. Nevertheless, at present there are no similar coordination and governance 
structures at the “lower”, basin and sub- basin levels, where the sustainable use and conservation of forest 
landscapes , the provision of environmental services, river basin management and hydrometeorological risks are 
dealt with in a comprehensive way. A new FMU approach that integrates forestry, sustainable territorial 
development within a watershed and climate change adaptation approach is needed. To advance towards this 
objective solid territorial governance and decision- making structures need to be in place within the FMUs, where 
all relevant institutions and actors contribute and take decisions in a coordinated and synergistic way. Inter -
institutional and multisectoral interventions to address climate change within an integrated territorial development 
approach will be one of the key innovations introduced by the Balsas Project, a paradigm changing innovation to be repl icated in other regions of the country. The project will support the creation of 10 basin, sub- basin and 
ethnic territories FMUs and the corresponding committees (RCIMTs ) through intensive technical and facilitation 
assistance that will be provided to CONAFOR´s local extension offices, the “ Promotorías Forestales ” by two 
specialists, a senior watershed planning and a junior social participation specialist, that will provide technical, 
communication and facilitation to support the formalization and official registration of these statutory entities.  
 59. Activity 1.1.2. Agree on priority intervention areas at the sub and micro- basin level.  Within each Regional 
Committee for the Integrated Management of the Territory (RCIMTs), and with project technical assistance and 
the cartographic support from CONAGUA´s, AGRICULTURA ´s and CONAFOR´s experts, priority intervention 
areas for landscape restoration and risk management within each basin, sub- basin and indigenous territory will 
be identified  through a bottom- up, participatory process . The prioritization criteria for both these dimensions, 
“landscape restoration need and potential”, as well as “risk management” shall be further developed within each 
RCIMT with the participation of stakeholders and included in an Operational Manual, in al ignment with the 
improved prioritization criteria to be implemented during project implementation. Nevertheless, the basic 
elements and dimensions for this prioritization are included in paragraph 62, namely: incorporating climate 
change dimensions for sus tainable forest landscape management and restoration; the potential for sustainable 
use, conservation and restoration of forests, its natural resources and environmental services, the identification 
of water supply and demand zones, among others. Detailed description of prioritization criteria for each of the 
incentive schemes of the PADFSB can be found in Sections 5.5.5 and 5.5.6 of the Feasibility Study, Annex 2.a 
to the FP; as well as in the Operation Rules of the PADFSB included in Section 6.  
60. Building on CONAFOR´s “Regional Forest Management Programs” (RFMP) methodology and taking on 
board relevant elements and lessons learnt of the “Integral Basin Management Action Plan” (PAMIC) 
methodology51 developed by the SEMARNAT and the National Commission of Natural Protected Areas 
(CONANP), a participatory territorial planning process will be facilitated. This participatory prioritization and 
planning process will incorporate climate change dimensions for sustainable forest landscape management and restoration at the basin and sub- basin levels. This participatory territorial planning instrument encompasses 
sustainable use, conservation and rest oration of forests, its natural resources and environmental services at the 
basin, sub- basin and ethnic territory levels. Project technical assistance will enable different stakeholders to 
develop land use zoning plans based on socio- economic and climate c hange vulnerabilities including higher 
rainfall  intensity , flash floods, soil erosion and landslides, increasing temperatures and droughts.  
61. Through this participatory territorial planning approach each basin, sub- basin and ethnic territory will be 
delimited into several prioritized micro- basins, where rural communities and municipal capitals share social and 
economic relations and together can take decisions related to the management of water, soil, forest, and other 
natural resources. This concept helps to identify water supply and demand zones and connects them through 
joint work with the municipalities, agrarian communities, and other state and federal government entities. 
Moreover, it harmonizes the cultural and the physical -natural areas that facilitate the social management of micro -


---
*Page 28*
---

B  
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 24 
 
 basins. In the Balsas PA, these prioritized areas may include three to five municipalities, and several agrarian 
communities.  
62. The main elements of a stepwise participatory territorial management plan are the following:  
● Identification of prioritized micro basins, in terms of hydrological functionalities and vulnerability 
to climate change  
● Assessment  of ecosystems, water supply and demand zones (ground and surface water supply 
and sediment retention potential); and identification of communities that have no supply from irrigation systems or local drinking water network s 
● Participatory validation  of technical models for sustainable management and conservation of 
ecosystems, developed according to the specific conditions of each basin and sub- basin and 
the most promising value chains in terms of the existing market, the inclusion of small holders , 
women, young and indigenous peoples, as well as the participation of the private sector (leading to Outcome  2) 
● Culturally appropriate, gender inclusive communication and awareness raising activities about 
the Project, its objective, and its approach  
● Assignment of responsibility to a Promotoría Forestal  to each RCIMT for facilitation support 
acting as technical secretariat  
● Discussion of proposal s to strengthen technical capacities and organization for forest 
firefighting and prevention, dealing with extreme hydrometeorological events, as well as 
adequate access to information systems (leading to Outcome 3) 
● Disseminate information about the incentive programs offered by CONAFOR, CONAGUA, 
AGRICULTURA  and BIENESTAR  
● Sharing of information about institutional programs and budgets to strengthen 
complementarities and synergies at all 3 levels of government, with the participation and 
oversight of local actors and the Agrarian Communities (leading to Activities 1.1.3 and 1.1.4)  
● Explore opportunities for involvement and additional support from other actors and the private 
sector.  
 
63. Activity 1.1.3. Strengthen multi sectoral coordination structures, Rules of Operation and mechanism for 
co-investment within the basins.  CONAFOR´s Promotorías forestales  will support and facilitate the planning and 
execution of participatory territorial management plans. For the development of participatory territorial 
management plans at each basin, sub- basin and ethnic territories, CONAFOR, CONAGUA, local governments, 
research centers, and other relevant institutional actors will collaborate through the corresponding RCIMTs to 
identify the prioritized actions and work plans and promote the participation of grassroots organizations, women, youth, indigenous peoples, and t he private sector. T raditional knowledge of agrarian communities and indigenous 
peoples for the sustainable management of the forest landscapes and the adoption of agroecological best 
practices will be identified and incorporated. The private sector and public companies will also be approached, 
and their active involvement sought (please see also Annex 7c  Stakeholder  Engagement Plan). Once a plan has 
been drafted, municipalities, agrarian communities, and grass -root organizations will be invited to prepare and 
submit their investment proposals to CONAFOR and CONAGUA to prepare more detailed investment plans to 
be financed by the inst itutional incentive programs PADFSB  and PROAGUA (refer to the activity 1.2.2). With 
project support and facilitation, it is expected that this collaborative and participatory planning process will improve inter-institutional and multisectoral coordination, foster the active participation of  grassroot organizations and 
Agrarian Communities, and lead to formal agreements (LoA´s, MoU´s and others) and an active involvement of 
the private sector. The preparation of proposals by groups with broad participation of women and young people 
will be en couraged and formulation supported. Private companies, municipalities and other state and non- state 
entities will be invited to participate in innovative financing mechanisms for forest landscape conservation,  like 
the Local Mechanisms for PES.  
 
64. Activity 1.1.4 Strengthen and operationalize criteria for adaptation to climate change in institutional 
program procedures.  The Rules of Operation ( “Reglas de Operación” ) that norm the PADFSB52 regulate the 
allocation of financial incentives for forest landscape restoration, forest management and value chain 
improvement. Its prioritization and eligibility criteria are based on socio- economic and ecological aspects. 
Nevertheless, these criteria do not always target the most vulnerable rural communities and groups such as 
women and youth and fail to include clear criteria for climate change adaptation that are oriented to improve 
current forest landscape restoration and forest management practices and at the same time, increase the 
livelihood resilience of the most vulnerable rural communities. Through this activity and with project technical 
assistance, CONAFOR and CONAGUA will adaptively improve , simplify , and adjust the operational procedures

 and criteria contained in the Reglas de Operación , based on the experience and the feedback from field 
operations, to make sure its operational procedures prioritize projects that clearly improve livelihood and

65. Activity 1.2.1 Communication and awareness raising campaign to disseminate calls for proposals.  While 
CONAFOR´s Promotorías forestales  disseminate the offer of PADFSB incentives, they do not have enough 
capacities and resources to reach all of the vast territory and the communities that they should be benefiting. 
Therefore, a strategy to target the prioritized watersheds and territories will be developed through an intensive communication and awareness raising campaign that has the objective to 
inform Agrarian and Indigenous 
Communities in these areas of the incentive programs and of their new modalities to improve resilience and 
adaptation to climate change. The campaign will be done through public websites and social media, but also in 
traditio nal communication forms in indigenous languages where appropriate, and specifically target women and 
youth. Climate change adaptation principles and criteria for landscape restoration practices will be 
communicated, CONAFOR and CONAGUA´s incentive schemes explained, and examples of investment models 
will be disseminated. Economic, financial, and social benefits of climate adapted practices will be explained, 
discussed, and improved with feedback from the local actors. It is expected that this activity will trigger an 
increased interest of the most vulnerable and marginalized ACs and its members to submit Project Profiles to be discussed and validated by the RCIMTs. The most vulnerable groups (women, youth,  and the indigenous 
population) will receive technical support and will have simplified formats for presenting their proposals.  
 66. Activity 1.2.2 Formulation of climate smart multi -annual investment plans based on micro- basin zoning 
plans and validated through FPIC. The Project Profiles submitted by AC´s will be analyzed by the RCIMTs and 
CONAFOR´s Promotorías  Forestales . Those that are aligned with the Regional Forest Management Programs 
(RFMPs), are located within prioritized intervention zones, include eligible activities, and comply with climate 
change adaptation and livelihood resilience criteria will be prioritized.  Special attention and support will be 
provided to proposals presented by groups of women, youth, and indigenous peoples. Once prioritized, 
CONAFOR, in accordance with the Rules for Operation of the PADFSB , will transfer financial resources to the 
benefitting organizations and AC´s, to recruit accredited technical service providers. As these beneficiaries recruit 
technical services by themselves, double- dipping of technical support services is not possible. Project Profiles 
will then be developed into full proposals to be financed by different incentive schemes of the PADFSB53 (please 
see detailed description of PADFSB  incentive schemes also in Outcome  2 and Annex 2c ), including local zoning 
plans and multi annual investment plans. Project experts will assist CONAFOR´s local technicians to evaluate 
proposals making sure that they are technically viable and economically sound, comply with climate change 
criteria and are subject to a Free, Prior and Informed Consultation (FPIC) process involving all relevant 
stakeholders.

68. Activity 1.3.2: Support and creation of forest protection brigades and climate change resilience facilitators.  
The project will support: i) the creation and strengthening of existing local forest protection brigades (forest fires, pests and diseases) and; ii) the incorporation of women, youth, and indigenous people as local facilitators who 
participate in actions oriented to improve climate change resilience. These groups will be created at local level to 
serve the 121 municipalities of the highest priority but should be able to be deployed also to the other 280 
municipalities of high priority within the Balsas PA. The Project will support their participation in the formulation 
of local land use plans and multi -annual investment plans, climate, and hazard risk management, as well as 
discussions to develop or improve local community’ by-laws. Project support will include uniforms, boots, tools, 
operational expenses for mobilization, control and monitoring rounds, and material for training and extension, among others. In both cases support by the project to these groups will have to be validated by the RCIMTs, 
under the facilitation and final decision of CONAFOR´s local Promotorías forestales . Positive action should be 
applied in both cases to secure the participation of women and young people. It is expected that every year 38 local facilitator groups and brigades will be supported.  
 
69. Activity 1.3.3: Strengthening of CONAFORs´ Promotorías Forestales . CONAFOR operates 26 regional 
and local offices in the Balsas Basin called “Promotorías forestales”  of which 7 are responsible to support 
sustainable forest development and forest landscape restoration in the prioritized basins, sub- basins and 
indigenous territories (see Figure 12). These promotorías  have very limited resources and capacities, which are 
insufficient to promote the paradigm shift that this project strives to bring about, and to intervene in the most 
socially and environmentally critical areas. Therefore, CONAFOR will commit substantial  public resources to 
strengthen these promotorías  through the recruitment of 26 additional local technicians, which will be trained and 
supported by the thematic specialists recruited by the Project. Merit, but also positive action should be practiced hiring  more women and local indigenous people that speak an indigenous language and are active in their 
communities , guaranteeing the presence of women in all promotorías  as a mechanism to facilitate the approach 
of Project initiatives to the female target population. Current and new personnel will be subject to intensive training and capacity development to put them in the capacity to use the training material, promote th
e effective 
participation of women, youth and Indigenous peoples, and to profit from experience exchanges, access to 
information platforms and early warning systems ( Outcome  3), as well as the use of safeguard instruments. The 
promotorías  will be provided with equipment and material to undertake their duties adequately financed by 
CONAFORs regular budget, which grants its sustainability and its maintenance in the long term.

70. Forest ecosystems and forest landscape that are crucial for the provision of environmental services and 
to sustain the livelihoods of the most vulnerable households  in the Balsas PA  have been subject to deforestation  
and forest degradation through unregulated over -exploitation, the forest fires and extensive overgrazing. As

governance and investment planning on the prioritized watersheds, this second outcome will provide the 
capacities and investments needed to implement climate adapted landscape restoration and conservation 
practices and systems , and will  to bring under sustainable management, conservation or restoration 324,578 ha 
of land, contributi ng to Target 5 on the U pdated Strategic Plan of the GCF for 2024 -2027.  
71. A fundamental paradigm shift that this project will bring about is that incentive payments that CONAFOR 
grants to Agrarian Communities through the PADFSB comply with principles, criteria and indicators for effective 
climate change adaptation and improved resilience of the most vulnerable households and communities. The 
sub-activities described below are examples of how PADFSB´s incentive payments can be transformed from 
BAU to finance for climate smart practices that support climate change adaptation of vulnerable communities and 
ecosystems. Nevertheless, as the payment of incentives follows a demand driven , bottom -up approach that

 responds to the needs and capacities of each Agrarian Community as project proponent, the models and

Output 2.1: Agrarian communities implement  land-use plans through multi -annual payment of 
environmental services (PES)  
72. Agrarian communities are owners and custodians of very large areas of forests and forest landscapes 
that provide essential ecosystem services for the Basin. Rainwater regulation, storage and erosion control is probably one of the most important ecosystem s ervices not only for the livelihood of Agrarian Communities, but 
also because the El Infiernillo  dam, which is the second most important for hydroelectric production in Mexico 
gets its water from the Balsas Basin. The forest landscapes and ecosystems of the Balsas Basin, but particularly 
their organic soil components need to be protected as they have  the capacity to store rainfall water and release 
it slowly, which is crucial vis a vis the increasing and more intense and erratic precipitation patterns that have 
been observed in the last decades, especially in the Sierra Madre del Sur and in the Sistema Neovolcánico 
Transversal mountain ranges . Ensuring forest landscape conservation is an effective adaptation strategy, which 
will be supported through PES to be financed through PADFSB  Component IV “Environmental Services for 
Wellbeing” ( SA.1.1 Servicios Ambientales ), as well as through Local PES Mechanisms LM- PES (SA2.MLPSA-
FC Mecanismos locales de pago por Servicios Ambientales a través de Fondos Concurrentes . Please see also 
Annex 2. a) The latter can involve state and municipal governments, the private sector , non-governmental 
organizations (NGO) and communities and will be supported and expanded as a result of Outcome 1 of this 
Project.  
 
73. Activity 2.1.1 Payment of Environmental Services (PES) . For the strategic areas for the provision of 
environmental services, particularly micro- watersheds identified within the participatory planning and decision-
making process promoted through the activities in Outcome 1, Agrarian Communities will be put in the capacities 
to present proposals and investment plans to CONAFOR to receive multi -annual PES financed by the PADFSB 
as to its Rules  for Operation. In contrast to past practices where only forest ecosystem protection was supported 
by CONAFOR and  bringing on board the innovative approach developed through its PROFOEM Project, 
CONAFOR will now finance PES not only for forest protection, but also conservation, sustainable use and 
restoration activities. This is a fundamental change. Additionally, AC  are now allowed to spend 50%54 of PES 
transfers in community development, women projects, traspatio improvements of other activities of their choice. 
Through this mechanism vital ecosystem services of forests and natural ecosystems on forest landscapes that 
are in threat of degradation through unsustainable use of fire and land use change / deforestatio n will be 
conserved. Additionally, these investments will leverage conservation finance from the private, state and NGO sector to be channeled through LM- PES that will secure the long-
term financial sustainability and smooth 
transition from catalytic publi c (PADFSB) to sustained long term regional and private finance (LM- PES). To be 
eligible, Agrarian Communities will have to comply with the eligibility , prioritization and exclusion55 criteria 
included in CONAFOR´s Rules  for Operation56 and include in their proposals for areas of reasonably well 
conserved forests and forest landscapes that will have to be prioritized by the RCIMT to secure adaptation 
benefits and alignment with the RFMPs.   
 
Output 2.2: The productive systems of Agrarian Communities are strengthened and better adapted to 
climate change and variability 
74. The main productive systems that sustain the livelihoods of rural households of Agrarian Communities in 
the project region are threatened by changes and variability of climate regimes as described in detail in Section 
B.1 above and in Annex 2b. Improvements to forestry and agricultural practices and management systems will be introduced and implemented by these 
communities to help them adapt better to significantly higher 
temperatures and the rise and variability of rainfall patterns to secure their livelihood resilience and to conserve vital ecosystem services. The expected main adaptation impact of the activiti es described below are the following: 
(i) reduce soil erosion to decrease sediments, improve water quality and diminish silting of watercourses and 
flooding; (ii) increase of the time that water remains within the landscape and the watershed, decreasing th e force 
and speed of runoff, as well as increasing infiltration; (iii) conserve soil for productive activities; (iv) moderate 
extreme temperatures thanks to vegetation coverage and tree shade.  
75. To this end, CONAFOR will grant incentive payments  to finance the investment plans developed by 
Agrarian Communities to cover costs for technical assistance, tools, and inputs, as also for labour and time 
invested by its members. These incentive payments  (“apoyos ”) will be done through the following components of 
the PADFSB (please see also Annex 2. a, Section 5.5. 6): a.) Forest Landscape Restoration of Micro- watersheds 
and Strategic Areas “Restauración Forestal de Microcuencas y Regiones Estratégicas”  (RFM); b.) Commercial 
Forest Plantations and Agroforestry Systems “Plantaciones Forestales Comerciales y Sistemas Agroforestales”


---
*Page 32*
---

B  
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 28 
 
 (PFC A); and c.) Community Forest Management and Value Chains “Manejo Forestal Comunitario y Cadenas de 
Valor”  (MFCCV). Through project activity 1.1.4 these incentive schemes of the PADFSB  , which pursue the 
objective of forest landscape conservation and the wellbeing (“para el Bienestar” ) will be improved to include 
climate adaptation criteria for enhanced ecosystem and livelihood resilience of vulnerable rural population.  
 
76. Activity 2.2.1 Climate Smart Forest Landscape Restoration of Micro- watersheds and Strategic Areas 
(RFM).  This activity has the objective to support members of Agrarian Communities to implement forest 
landscape restoration practices on degraded areas to recover soil and ecosystem productivity, generate 
employment and wellbeing and increase ecosystem services and livelihood resilience. The following activities 
exemplify three possible soil and water conservation options out of a multiplicity of variants and their 
combinations, that members of Agrarian Communities may want to include in their Proposals and Investment 
Plans to be submitted to CONAFOR for financial support.  
 77. Sub-activity 2.2.1.1 Establish climate resilient agroforestry systems . Cocoa and coffee are important cash 
crops that sustain vulnerable rural livelihoods in the project area, both highly dependent on ambient temperatures 
and rainfall patterns. Moreover, high value Arabica coffee, especially the type that meets the qualifications of 
more lucrative specialty markets, requires lower temperatures, as higher ambient temperatures speed up the 
ripening of coffee berries, leading to poorer cup quality. Rainfall in the Balsas PA will increase and be erratic, 
exhibiting greater extremes, which will significantly impact coffee, whose production cycle is highly dependent on 
rainfall patterns. Coffee flowering is triggered by the first rainfalls at the onset of the rainy season, but if 
precipitation drops off or becomes too heavy, both co ffee flowers and fruits may drop from the tree. This stunted 
fruit growth would result in fewer, smaller beans of lower quality, which would in turn fetch lower prices57. 
Additionally, higher temperature and humidity are expected to increase the spreading of phytopathogens and 
disease development, such as coffee rust, especially at higher altitudes58. 
 
78. Through this activity the agrarian communities  will be put in the capacity to restore degraded forest 
landscapes and pastures into biodiverse coffee and/or cocoa agroforestry systems (AFS). They will be provided with the financial resources  to procure technical assistance from CONAFOR´s accredited service providers, and 
to buy inputs and materials to establish AFS and restore degraded pasture, rangeland, or renew old degraded 
orchards  with varieties better adapted to the changing climate. One of several possible highly biodiverse tree / 
crop combination within an AFS as presented in Annex 2a, Chapter 3, would include corn intercropped with 3 
500 arabica coffee and 467 cocoa plants, 350 cardamom plants, 35 banana and 66 melliferous timber trees or other fruit trees in an area of one hectare. Of especial relevance is the introduction of improved crop varieties more adapted to rising temperatures and heavier rainfall, and more resistant to pest and diseases, with higher productivity and high organoleptic quality. The combination of banana, fruit and native timber tree species and 
annual crops create a highly biodiverse agroecosystem that will sequester more carbon, regulate the micro-climate, and lower ambient temperature as a result of increased shade. I will protect the soil and water sources, 
enhance family asset due to timber tree species and ensure food security through crop diversification. Overall, 
integrative management of AFS will improve social, economic, and environmental sustainability. In addition to 
these  adaptation benefits the GHG mitigation potential of this AFS has been estimated to be -  5.8 t CO
2 eq/ha/year.  
 
79. Sub-activity 2.2.1.2 Establish agave –  forest water / soil retention terraces . Increasing and more intense 
rainfall on heavily degraded areas located on moderate to steep slopes are subject to increased soil erosion and 
the formation of gullies and ravines. Unregulated grazing,  exploitation of remaining trees for firewood and 
predatory removal  of vegetation, including wild agaves , exacerbate the degradation process caused by extreme 
rainfall and rising temperatures, causing barrages, catastrophic flashfloods, and landslides. Landscape 
restoration practices to slow down runoff, enhance rainwater infiltration,  retention and storage in the soil, as well 
as the formation of natural terraces along the contour lines are needed to restore landscapes and ecosystem 
services.  
80. Approximately 50 m wide fringes of densely planted agave (incl. A. cupreata, A. augustifolia and others) 
will be established along the contour lines, followed up- hill with fringes of biodiverse, naturally regenerated forests 
(50 m) and enrichment plantings of native tree species ( Cedro,  Tepeguaje, Guachichimil, Encino, Chiquil,  others ). 
These fringes will be protected through fences during the first 5 years to prevent goats and cattle to destroy the 
young growing tree seedlings and the agave plants. After this time  the densely planted agave fringes, once big 
enough, will form thorny fences that will hinder goats and cattle to enter the naturally regenerating forest fringes. As agaves mature irregularly, proper management of regeneration and selective harvesting will assu
re the 
permanence of these natural agave barriers. Over time the agave fringes will retain and accumulate sediments 
and organic matter which will form natural terraces contour lines  against the slope. These natural terraces will


---
*Page 33*
---

B  
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 29 
 
 slow down the runoff during extreme rainfall events, diminish ing soil erosion and improv ing water infiltration, 
storage and allow for slow water release.  
81. As documented in Annex 2 the ample offer of products from the wild agave species (syrup, fibres, 
artisanal mezcal, others) have a rising demand and are important as income source for households of Agrarian 
Communities. On the other hand, timber and especially firewood that will be produced in the naturally regenerated 
and sustainably managed forest fringes are important for the provision of timber and non- timber forest products 
(fibres, species, medicinal herbs, aromatic essences , among others ), and especially firewood for household use 
and to supply the demand for  artisanal mezcal production. The adaptation benefits of landscape restoration 
through natural fringes and terraces for water regulation has  an important GHG mitigation benefit that has been 
estimated at -26.2 t CO 2eq/ha/year.  
 82. Sub-activity 2.2.1.3 Establish forest water/soil retention terraces . This sub-activity parts from a similar 
baseline situation as the one described above of degraded areas located on moderate to steep slopes to be 
restored to forest landscapes for sub- activity 2.2.1.2: The difference consists in the interest of AC and the local 
markets conditions to plant, process and sell agave; or to rather continue with livestock activities and to transform 
them into a sustainable, carbon neutral income source. To this end a transformation from extensive unregulated 
grazing, to regulated rotational grazing on areas of communal use will be incentivized . To achieve this, 
approximately 50 m wide fringes of forests will be established through natural regeneration and enrichment 
plantings along the contour lines against the slope, leaving 100 m wide fringes of fenced pastureland free between 
the next forest fringe, again 50 m wide. These forest fringes will accumulate the sediments and organic matter 
transported downhill by the water flows foreseen due to increasing and more extreme precipitation, therefore 
improve rainfall infiltration in the soil, and successively form natural terraces. Native tree species to be used 
include Tepeguaje, Guachichimil, Encino, Chiquil,  Cahuesquitl, Guaje Leucaena sp and others. Enrichment 
plantings and management of Bursera linaloe to produce olinalá essence, Cuachalalate and other medicinal 
plants and non- timber forest products (Orégano, Palma soyate, palma dulce)  will generate additional income 
opportunities , especially to benefit women.  
83. The separated and individualized fringes of grassland will allow for better livestock management by 
through rotating grazing with mobile electric fen ces operated with mobile solar panels. Rotational grazing 
produces less soil compaction and allows the pasture ecosystem to regenerate during the time it is not used. 
Dispersed naturally regenerated native trees within the pastures fringes (including samán, pochote, colorín, 
native guaje and other Leucaena species , mezquite, huizatche) further improve the soil through nitrogen fixation 
and gradually incorporate nutrients into the soil -grass system59. Furthermore, these trees enhance the nutrition 
of the herd through protein rich pods, providing shade that improve animal wellbeing and productivity. In dry 
tropical climate regions in Colombia which are similar to the Intermontane Depressions (ID) of the Balsas PA, 
Murgueitio et al60 found that the presence of shade trees in appropriate density in silvo- pastoral systems reduce 
high temperatures and maintain soil moisture which resulted in reduction of the average annual temperature 
between 2- 3 degrees  Celsius , and had a cooling effect of up to 13 degrees in the hottest days. In addition, relative 
humidity increased by 10- 20% and evapotranspiration was reduced by approximately 1.8 mm/day. This is 
particularly relevant in the ID Ecoregion of the Basin PA where extensive livestock herding is practiced , and 
already very high mean annual temperatures are expected to increase even more from the year 2041 onwards.  
84. GHG emission from this semi extensive silvopastoral production system is mitigated through tree growth 
of the forest fringes and biomass accumulation: the net GHG sequestration has been estimated at - 26.2 t CO 2eq 
/ ha / year. The additional adaptation benefits as well as the environmental, economic, and social benefits from 
sustainable silvopastoral systems ( SPS)  are documented in Annex 2a.  
 
85. Activity 2.2.2 Commercial Forest and Agroforestry Plantations Adapted to Climate Change (PFC A). While 
Activity 2.2.1 (RFM) has the objective to restore degraded forest landscapes, this activity aims to improve climate 
change adaptation and the productive potential of commercial forest plantations and agroforestry systems and to 
increase the sustainable production of forest timber and non- timber forest products.  
 
86. Sub-activity 2.2.2.1 Establish climate adapted planted forests (PFC A.1). Due to climate change and 
variability, forest managers seek to generate tools to prepare the forests they manage for a future climate that 
may be very different from the one in which these forests were established. The principles and strategies61 for 
Adaptive Silviculture explained further below will help maintain production and ecological balance, especially in productive ecosystems that will not be able to be maintained under future climatic conditions. Adaptive silviculture 
takes advantage of the different coping strategies with which ecosystems deal with changes
62, among the main 
ones are:  
● Resistance: the capacity of forests to maintain conditions relatively unchanged, this capacity is 
useful when ecosystems face small changes. Forest managers can help forest’s resistance by 
improving their defenses maintaining the original ecosystem conditions 63.


---
*Page 34*
---

B  
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 30 
 
 ● Resilience : the ability of forests to return to the previous condition after a medium size 
disturbance, such as wildfire, deforestation, natural pests, or not too extreme climate changes. 
To improve the resilience more diversity and changes of composition of the forest  species 
should be considered.  
● Response options : if climate changes or disturbances are strong and constant, ecosystems can 
make a transition and respond to the new conditions. In this case foresters should manage 
ecosystems to adapt to the new conditions.  
87. Adaptive forestry is the combination of applied ecology with applied genetics that seeks to foster a system 
to address the functionality of forest ecosystems under conditions of environmental change to meet future needs. 
The most important components of adaptive silviculture are:  
● Tree density management : having an optimal density reduces risks such as wildfires, water 
stress by reducing competition for water and nutrients between trees. It is also important to have 
control over the density of shrubs, as this can interfere with natural regeneration and can be 
combustible material which may increase the risk to forest fires64. 
● Stand composition management : maintaining tree diversity, in terms of species, age and size65, 
adding species that are better adapted to future conditions 66, or more resilient. Planting a wider 
variety of species and genotypes allows expanding the genetic pool and therefore, improving the adaptive capacity of the forest.  
● Assisted migration of species  that do not inhabit the area, but are very likely to do so, due to 
future conditions67. 
● Threat control : through activities such as cutting and controlled “cold” burning it is possible to 
limit the effect caused by biotic threats such as pests, diseases, and invasive species; or 
removing physical threats such as ignition sources68. 
88. Symptoms of forest decline have been detected in the forests of the Trans -Mexican Volcanic Belt of the 
Balsas Basin and northwestern temperate forest of Mexico due to an increase in temperature and a decrease in 
precipitation causing a significant loss of habitat for Mexico´s conifer and pine- oak biomes. Furthermore, as a 
collateral effect of climate change, the populations of Pinus hartwegii  have been affected by different species of 
mistletoe, and the populations of Abies religiosa , of the Monarch Butterfly Natural Reserve and threatened 
species like Picea martinezii  T.F. Patt., and Picea mexicana have reduced their size drastically69. 
89. Together with national academic research partners, including the Forest Faculty of the University of 
Durango, the project will support Agrarian Communities to establish and to manage forests applying the principles 
and the management practices of Adaptive Silviculture mentioned above, support the adaptive adjustment of the 
Rules for Operation of CONAFOR´s PADFSB and to monitor its impacts. This will be also supported with intensive 
training and capacity building of CONAFOR´s local technicians of the Promotorías Forestales , technical service 
providers, forest managers and members of Agrarian Communities, the forest protection brigades, local leaders 
and facilitators. The mitigation impact of climate adapted forest plantations in the Balsas PA has been estimated 
to be - 20.1 t CO 2eq / ha / year.  
 
90. Sub-activity 2.2.2.2 Establish climate- smart silvopastoral systems (PFC A.2). In contrast with the pasture-
forest fringes  described for Sub-activity 2.2.1.3, which is designed for extensive degraded areas of communal 
use land of Agrarian Communities, this sub- activity consists in supporting individual families or groups of ACs to 
improve their livestock management on areas of individual or family use of an area of at least 20 ha / household 
located on moderate to low slopes, in relative proximity to the house, settlement, or town. These systems can 
therefore be managed in a much more intense way.  
91. The baseline situation is extensively managed pasture land with very few or no shade trees that is at risk 
of further degradation through soil compaction, erosion and consequently, low rainwater infiltration leading to the 
formation of ravines and gullies. Foreseen higher precipitation volumes increase the risk for landslides and flash floods. On the ot her hand, low feed quality contributes to higher methane emissions from enteric fermentation 
while carbon storage into the aerial and soil biomass decreases as soils are being degraded by mismanagement, 
higher temperatures, and more erratic precipitation p atterns.  
92. The specific adaptation benefits of silvopastoral systems for the project region are similar to those 
described above for Activity 2.2.1.3, which are the following : i.) Separated and individualized fringes of grassland 
will allow for better livestock management by rotating the herd between different fringes /or their compartments 
formed by mobile electric feces ; ii.) Rotational grazing produces less soil compaction and allows the pasture 
ecosystem to regenerate during the time it is not used  and; iii.) Dispersed naturally regenerated native trees within 
the pastures fringes (including saman, pochote, colorín, native guaje and other Leucaena species, mezquite, huizatche) further improve the soil through nitrogen fixation and gradually incorporate nutrients into the soil -grass 
system
70. Furthermore, these trees enhance the nutrition of the herd through protein rich pods, providing shade 
that improve animal wellbeing and productivity.


---
*Page 35*
---

B  
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 31 
 
 93. Silvo -pastoral systems in combination with improved forage  grass es increase high quality feed 
availability, thus lower methane emissions, allow for soil restoration, increase resilience to extreme weather 
events (drought, excess rainfall), provide firewood and contribute to household food security. Apart from providing 
shade and animal feed, the trees provide additional income (which can be substantial) through the sale of timber 
of valuable trees like guanacaste (Enterolobium cyclocarpum) , ramón  or tillo blanco (Brosimum alicastrum) , cedro  
(Cedrela odorata) , among various others. Because of the importance of livestock production in the Balsas PA, 
massive adoption can have a profound impact, if some rather basic improvements to actual BAU practices are 
introduced.  
94. As there are multiple alternatives and combinations of the before mentioned elements for a SPS which 
depend on the local condition and the preferences of each farmer, one SPS model with its technical specifications  
and an economic and financial analysis specifically developed and proposed by Simmonit et al. (2020) for the 
project region is included in Annex 2 71. It is estimated that family income will increase up to 2.5 times within the 
lifespan of these productive projects and that the net GHG mitigation potential of the introduction of silvo- pastoral 
systems will be approximately - 13.25 t CO 2eq / ha / year.  
 
Output 2.3: Participation of Agrarian Communities and Producer Organizations in forest and agroforestry 
value chains is strengthened . 
95. Activity 2.3.1  Sustainable Forest Management adapted to climate change, inclusion, and access to 
markets (MFCCV).  This Component of the PADFSB  has the objective to support Agrarian Communities that own 
natural forests to implement actions that enable them to strengthen their forest governance, including their social, 
technical and cultural capacities to manage, utilize and market their forest re sources in a sustainable way. 
Activities will include elements to make traditional sustainable forest management better adapted to climate change and promote greater inclusion and empowerment of the most vulnerable, women and youth in production 
processes as well as inclusive, fair trade and certified value chains.  
 
96. Sub-activity 2.3.1.1 Manage forests sustainably adapted to climate change (MFCCV.1).  During the 
project appraisal mission several Agrarian Communities that were visited and local CONAFOR technicians 
reported that natural forests are under stress due to climate change and variability as resin production has 
declined or had climate related yield variability. Resin yield of Pinus oocarpa  and pseudostrobus  forests being 
traditionally harvested by Agrarian Communities are highly sensitive to temperature extremes and show 
increased susceptibility to pests and diseases, particularly to bark beetles (Dendroctonus sp.) , which have already 
led to catastrophic forest loss in broad areas of Central America, particularly in neighboring Guatemala and 
Honduras. On the other hand, natural forest of Bursera linaloe  traditionally used by Agrarian Communities for 
their aromatic timber and aromatic oil had much lower seed production in years of longer dry periods and years 
with irregular rainfall.  
97. Through this sub- activity the project will support Agrarian Communities to plan forest management, 
strengthen governance structures and to implement cultural and harvesting practices applying principles, 
methods and best practices of adaptive silviculture described for Sub- activity 2.2.2.1 The objective is to promote 
sustainable forest management that improves the resilience of the forest ecosystems which are an important source of income for the most vulnerable rural households and on the other side provide vital ecosystem services, 
especially water regulation and soil protection. Furthermore, the project will support community forest monitoring 
and impact evaluation for this activity, in collaboration with academic partners as the Forest Faculty of the 
University of Durango.  
98. Agrarian Communities will be supported with incentive payments included in PADFSB  MFCCV.1 to obtain 
technical support from CONAFOR´s accredited technical assistance service providers, to formulate or improve forest management plans, acquire tools and inputs, pay for the labour invested and strengthen their organization and technical ca pacities to undertake climate adapted, sustainable management and harvesting of timber and 
non-timber products.  
99. Apart from evident adaptation benefits, resin (rosin) production will be maintained, and climate adapted 
diversified sustainable forest management will produce additional timber and firewood that will increase income 
for Agrarian Communities. As the baseli ne situation in this case is traditionally managed natural forests, the 
additional mitigation impact of this activity has been estimated to be of only - 0.88 t CO2eq. / ha / year.  
 
100. Sub-activities 2.3.1.2 and 2.3.1.3 Sustainable use and added value to timber and to non- timber forest 
products (MFCCV. 2). Agrarian Communities in the Project Area frequently depend on the use and 
commercialization of forest products to sustain their livelihoods. For produce sold in raw form or poorly 
transformed low prices are obtained and the producers stay at the lowest li nk of the supply chain. This often 
causes  overexploitation and forest degradation, putting vulnerable rural households and forest ecosystems in 
even greater peril and perpetuating the poverty -resources degradation vicious cycle. To overcome marked


---
*Page 36*
---

B  
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 32 
 
 access burdens, financial incentives and technical assistance will be provided to Agrarian Communities and 
producer organizations to undertake strategic planning, organization, value chain development and marketing for 
sustainably harvested timber and non- timber forest products. Supply, processing , marketing and certification of 
forest products will also be supported with the objective to access more sophisticated, better paying markets for 
forest products with a low carbon footprint and socially responsible, fair traded production process. Since va lue 
addition activities are an important space for the participation of women, the provision of financial incentives and 
technical assistance to groups with broad female participation will be encouraged and prioritized.  
 
101. Sub-activity 2.3.1.4 Support women and youth in benefitting from forest -based value chains (MFCCV. 3). 
Financial and technical support will be provided specifically to trigger entrepreneurship and projects designed, 
implemented, and led by groups of women and youth. This specific funding window of the PADFSB will support 
women- managed productive projects and enterprises with the objective to support the empowerment of women 
within forest based productive processes and strengthen their voice and decision making within their 
communities. These projects may invo lve any step within the forest production chain, as well as any product and 
service development based on the sustainable use of forests and forest landscapes. These initiatives may include  
the use of pine woods, pine resin, olinalá  essence (Bursera linaloe) , oak charcoal, palm to produce handicrafts 
(Brahea dulcis) , a diversity of fruits, aromatic plants and medicinal herbs (including guachalalate, damiana, 
orégano ) among others. Some groups of women in the Project Area have developed nurseries to produce 
seedlings for forest and fruit trees, as well as non- timber forest plants like native agaves . The project will search 
to improve and scale- up these initiatives  as the demand for seeds and seedlings with a wider genetic diversity 
and resilience for climate adapted silviculture will rise ; as also to support landscape restoration terraces with 
agave and native tree saplings . Projects will be implemented under PMFSB  incentive line MFCCV.3.1 for 
productive projects for women and youth which  additionally  support s women group initiatives to lighten the burden 
of care and household tasks, so that women can dedicate more time to these productive activities.  
 
102. Sub-activity 2.3.1.5 Capacity building for local producers in sustainable forest management adapted to 
climate change (MFCCV. 4). In complementarity with the activities to improve sustainable production of forest 
goods and services, transformation and added value of forest raw materials members of Agrarian Communities 
and Producer Organizations will receive intensive training and capacity building. Training in gender and inclusion 
will be part of the themes to create socio- cultural spaces that are more receptive to the participation of women 
and youth. In case of women, the strengthening of capacities in organizational, managerial and marketing aspects 
is also contemplated. This training will involve courses within the Agrarian Communities, exchanges of 
experiences between Agrarian Communities and forest practitioners and groups of women to share best practices and experiences, as wel l as scholarships for community members.  
 103. Activity 2.3.2 Support households to improve food security and nutrition. Higher temperatures and more 
erratic rainfall patterns that have been identified in the project region pose and additional hazard to these already highly vulnerable communities that can be mitigated through targeted strategies to improve the adaptation of the 
traspatio (backyard) and the milpa
72 food production systems, which are the main sources of food for these 
vulnerable households.  
 
104. Sub-activities 2.3.2.1 – 2.3.2.3 Traditional milpa (maize/beans) and traspatio orchards are transformed 
to diversified milpa with trees, improved irrigation and water solutions  for improved food quality and nutrition.  
Vulnerable households will be supported in improving the milpa  and traspatio house- near orchards through 
targeted technical assistance for women, youth, and indigenous peoples , to build capacities in food security and 
nutrition, conserve and multiply native varieties, introduce new crops more resistant to higher temperatures, 
drought and flooding, (e.g., pithaya, amaranthus, aloe vera , others), increase the crop diversity to reduce 
dependence and spread risks, and introduce more fruit and firewood producing trees. Other measures to increase 
the livelihood resilience and reduce resources use include water solutions like drop irrigation,  rainwater harvesting 
solutions and improved cooking stoves that consume less firewood.  
105. The project will specifically support the improvement of the traditional milpa  (maize and beans) and 
traspatio production system into diversified agroforestry adapted to climate change. A basic but fundamental 
technological innovation to be introduced is that furrows will be established along the contour lines to reduce soil 
erosion and water run- off from rain that is increasing in volume and intensity. Additionally, a wide diversity of fruit 
trees (e.g., lemon in warmer zones, peaches in the mountains) and other native trees (firewood) will be planted 
every 10 meters along the contour lines to form  natural terraces which will improve soil formation and protection, 
nutrient cycling, and rainwater infiltration. Additional specific technical assistance will be provided to support the 
participatory design and implementation of water harvesting and stora ge solutions, including collector roofs, 
collector tanks ( “sombreritos ”) and reservoirs , among others. Due to the relevance of women in traspatio


---
*Page 37*
---

B  
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 33 
 
 production, they will be the main stakeholders and recipients of the services and infrastructures that the project 
will support.  
 
106. Activity 2.3.3. Brigades to strengthen adaptation capacity in Agrarian Communities (Community Forest 
Monitoring).  Traditionally the measurement for the National Forest Inventory and for the National Measurement, 
Reporting and Verification System (MRV) for REDD+ has been outsourced by CONAFOR to private service 
providers. This activity has the objective to pilot community forest resources monitoring to empower the Agrarian 
Communities not only to manage their forest resources sustainably, but also to monitor the forest cover and the 
forest condition. To this e nd, a total of 38 community forest brigades (MRV and forest protection/adaptation) will 
be formed in the project region that will be put together of 154 community members with a strong participation of 
women and young people.  
 
 
Outcome  3: The producers, inhabitants and authorities of the Balsas PA have timely and relevant climate 
information for climate risk management, disaster prevention and knowledge management (USD 3´648 
721) 
 
107. Output 3.1: Climate hazards related early warning and monitoring systems supported. The main 
institutions working in the Balsas PA (CONAFOR, CONAGUA, and AGRICULTURA ) have been developing 
advanced information systems related to weather forecast, forest fires, medium/long term evolution of climate 
patterns and climate risk prevention. However, climate information is hosted in different systems and platforms 
that are not yet interconnected. On the other hand, this information is accessed and used mainly by research and 
national institutes (e.g., Instituto Nacional de Investigaciones Forestales, Agrícolas y Pecuarias -  INIFAP) and is 
not easily accessible to communities, municipalities, extension services and to the leaders and households of 
Agrarian Communities.  
108. Activity 3.1.1 Development of a platform and applications to facilitate access to climate information and 
early warning systems. The Project will support the development of an online platform to facilitate access of 
vulnerable communities, extension workers, the Promotorías forestales , municipalities and other regional and 
local actors to existing information on weather forecasts and early warning about hazards from extreme / 
anomalous climate events to improve their readiness and preparedness. To this end, an initial participatory 
diagnostic of the regional and local reach and the barriers of the existing climate information systems and risk 
management of the National Disaster Prevention System CENAPRED will be carried out to understand to what 
extent it is used by municipalities, Agrarian Communities, indigenous communities, and households in the project region, considering the importance of women having access to the information provided through the platform. As 
a result, an online digital platform and its applications will be designe d in coordination between CENAPRED, 
CONAGUA and CONAFOR. The platform and its applications will be disseminated at regional level, and 
capacities of the members of local institutions, Agrarian Communities and households will be strengthened to 
manage agroc limatic risks, including the use of participatory mechanisms like “talking” agroclimatic risk maps, 
planting and harvesting calendars, and participatory vulnerability analysis tools. Considering the availability of 
mobile phone by the population, access to the platform will be promoted through these devices to facilitate access 
for women and young people. The experiences of participatory Agroclimatic Roundtables and Agroclimatic 
Bulletins  piloted in the State of Chiapas will be replicated and scaled up in the project area. This process will be 
done in close coordination with the capacity building activities 1.3.1, 1.3.2 and 1.3.3 involving the brigades and 
the local facilitators, including groups of women and youth, in coordination with the regional and local risk 
management entities (CENAPRED). Local committees / brigades and extension workers will be supported to 
draft contingency plans to deal with climate related emergencies and disasters, response preparedness and 
management at community level, as well as to use the community tools for the evaluation of damages and losses.  
109. Activity 3.1.2 Strengthen CONAFORs Forest Fire Prediction and Prevention (SPPIF) and Forest 
Phytosanitary Surveillance (SIVICOFF) Systems.  As in other regions of the North American West, forest fires 
pose an increasing risk to natural forest ecosystems and communities in Mexico and in the Balsas Basin. This is 
clearly due to higher temperatures that, especially during the dry season, increase the susceptibility and exposure 
to forests fires.  The Project will strengthen the existing Forest Fire Prediction and Prevention System (SPPIF) to 
make it more reliable and effective to provide timely information and generate data on areas at higher exposure, 
risks and on burned areas. A diagnostic of existent resources and capacities will be carried out and 
supplementary  IT equipment procured to make sure that the information generated by the SPPIF and the 
SIVICOFF can be accessed and used by the local brigades (see also Activity 2.3.3).  
 
110. Activity 3.1.3 Strengthen CONAFOR´s Measurement, Reporting and Verification System (MRV). The 
Project will strengthen the existing MRV System through additional GIS specialists and acquisition of equipment.


---
*Page 38*
---

B  
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0  | PAGE 34 
 
 The MRV field activities that traditionally have been outsourced by CONAFOR to consultant s and technical 
service providers will take a new approach and will be contracted to A grarian Communities  to undertake 
community forest monitoring. Community forest monitoring will help to empower community members in the 
sustainable use and conservation of their forest resources and include especially women and the youth. This pilot 
to be tested in the Balsas PA will involve local facilitators that will support Agrarian Communities to form the forest monitoring brigades described in Activity 2.3.3. This approach of community forest monitoring with support of 
local facilitators and brigades will be piloted and tested in the Balsas PA, and lessons learnt capitalized to up-
scale community forest monitoring to other States of the Mexican Federation.  CONAFOR’s MRV  System will be 
strengthened with IT equipment. Community Forest Monitoring Brigades will be supported by the purchase of 
vehicles and field equipment for forest measurement (including notebooks, GPS, measuring tapes, other). This 
equipment will be kept an d used at CONAFOR Promotorías Forestales and maintained as per CONAFORs 
standard equipment maintenance protocols after project completion.  
 111. Activity 3.1.4 Strengthen CONAFOR´s social and environmental safeguards management system.  While 
environmental and social safeguards are fully integrated within CONAFORs rules and procedures, its 
understanding and operational implementation still needs to be reinforced, especially at the level of its field offices, the Promotorías forestales . The project will provide intensive training and capacity building to CONAFORs 
Promotorías forestales , but also to the technical service providers and 
local facilitators to make sure 
environmental and social safeguards, and especially the project Gender Action Plan and the Indigenous Peoples 
Planning Framework IPPF are understood and duly implemented.  
 
Output 3.2: Policy -relevant knowledge products from the systematic documentation of good practices 
and intervention approaches of the project  
112. Activity 3.2.1 Production of training and systematization materials. With project support material for 
capacity development, knowledge products and communication products will be developed. Relevant themes 
include, but will not be limited to basin intervention approach, forestry adapted to climate change and adaptation 
mode ls, forest ecosystem monitoring system in the field, gender empowerment and youth inclusion, among 
others. Successful experiences will be systematized and documented in at least 4 relevant knowledge products 
to be disseminated at the regional and national level and will also be available on CONAFORs webpage, including successful experiences of women in climate resilient activities, relevant for the exchange of knowledge, the 
adoption of new policies and institutional strategies.  
 
113. Activity 3.2.2. Exchange of experiences, and South- South and Triangular Cooperation (SSTC).
 The 
Project will facilitate the organization of local, national and international exchanges of experiences on themes 
including, but not limited to adaptative forestry and silviculture, indigenous knowledge on forestry and food 
production, local vulnerability assessment, basin management, information systems and community MRV, 
inclusive values chains for women and youth t o foster the exchange of experiences between different Agrarian 
Communities and actors in the Balsas PA. Face to face visits and virtual experience exchange will be facilitated, 
as also, if required, South- South exchange events with other countries facilit ated and supported.  
 
114. Activity 3.2.3 Monitoring and evaluation.  Monitoring, reporting and evaluation will be conducted in 
accordance with IFAD and GCF procedures by the project team. A project -specific monitoring, evaluation and 
reporting system will be set -up by the PMU to carry out regular project monitoring activities, as also midterm and 
final project evaluations. A M&E specialist will be recruited to provide overall coordination  of monitoring, 
evaluation and reporting related activities , and an external mid- term and final evaluation team hired at the end of 
the Project. The possibility of incorporating the IFAD women empowerment index for measuring results on women empowerment will be considered

 115. The institutional arrangements for decision -making and project operational guidance have been 
developed based on discussions with the CONAFOR, the National Water Commission (“CONAGUA”), the 
Ministry of Finance (“HACIENDA”), and the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development (“AGRICULTURA”) 
under the guidance of the Office for the Coordination of Advisers to the Presidency of the Republic ( Coordinación 
de Asesores de la Presidencia de la República - CAP). The CAP will support CONAFOR in facilitating dialogue 
and coordination with other institutions at the federal and state levels, such as the Ministry of Environment and 
Natural Resources (“SEMARNAT”), which will support CONAFOR in formalizing agree ments with other 
institutions or ministries.  
116. For the financing of the Project, GCF will enter into a Funded Activity Agreement  (“FAA” , trust account) 
with IFAD, whereby the nature of the funding for the Project will be a combination of GCF Loans , notably a 50/50 
mix of a loan with high concessionality  (‘GCF Loan A ”) and a loan with low concessionality (“GCF Loan B”)  6,  
and a GCF Grant under which IFAD, in its capacity as Accredited Entity (the “AE”), shall administer the relevant GCF Proceeds.  
117. IFAD will, therefore, channel such GCF Proceeds through the Executing Entities (EEs – see below), in 
accordance with the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) , the FAA  and in line with the terms and conditions 
broadly described in the Term Sheet.  
118. IFAD will enter into the following subsidiary agreements : 
i) one Subsidiary Agreement with the Ministry of Finance (“HACIENDA”) for the GCF Loan s (the “Subsidiary Loan 
Agreement”). HACIENDA will subsequently enter into a Mandate and Implementation Agreement to assign: a) 
Nacional Financiera, S.N.C., Banca de Desarrollo (“NAFIN”) to act as Intermediary on behalf of the Federal 
Government, which will serve as the Executing Entity ("EE2") and the fiduciary of the Project ’s loans  b) The 
National Forestry Commission (“CONAFOR”) to act as the executing entity for the project, serving as the 
Executing Entity (“EE3”) with overall responsibility for project execution and coordination.  By designated authority 
from HACIENDA, NAFIN will channel funds to CONAFOR upon request. NAFIN will administer the GCF Loan s 
but will not execute any specific activities.  
ii) one Subsidiary Agreement with CONAFOR for the GCF Grant (the “Subsidiary Grant Agreement”). CONAFOR 
will act as the EE and will bear overall responsibility for the execution and coordination of the Project and will 
create a sub- account in the Fondo For estal Mexicano (“FFM”) to manage the GCF Grant.  
119. Under both Subsidiary Agreements, CONAFOR will undertake all operational responsibility for the 
implementation of the Project in the country.  
120. The Subsidiary Agreements shall detail specific roles and responsibilities of the relevant ministries 
involved in the implementation of the Project  (CONAFOR) and in the channeling of funds ( CONAFOR for the 
grant and NAFIN  and HACIENDA for the loans ). The Subsidiary Agreements shall establish the terms for the 
financing and implementation of Outcomes, Outputs, and Activities, and include the relevant obligations of the 
Executing Entities in respect of the implementation of the overall Funded Activity,  including compliance with the 
eligibility criteria (as set out in Annex II, the “Eligibility Criteria”) and the Project Implementation Manual (the 
“PIM”). Further details on the Subsidiary Agreements and other implementation arrangements are set out in the 
Term Sheet.  
121. The Subsidiary Agreements will be legally binding and will pass down the GCF policy requirements as 
set out in the FAA.  CONAFOR will select the beneficiaries of all Activities of the Project (the “Final Beneficiaries”) and procure service providers for specific Project Activities in accordance with the Eligibility Criteria and the PIM 
and subject to IFAD’s no objection
7 in accordance with the provisions of the Subsidiary  Agreement s. CONAFOR 
will enter into contracts for procured services with the selected services providers, subject to IFAD’s no objection 
in accordance with the provisions of the Subsidiary Agreements.

HACIENDA ( Executing Entity 1 –  “EE1” ) 
123. HACIENDA  is the political and operational government focal point for  international climate finance 
agencies. It also administers and defines the federal budget and expenditures for implementing public  programs, 
including those related to climate.  The mandate and implementation contract is a legal contract required by the 
Mexican legislation by which HACIENDA grants the mandate to a Development Banking Institution (in the case 
of Balsas: NAFIN), to act on behalf of the Federal Government as Intermed iary between the International 
Financial Institutions (in the case of Balsas: IFAD) and the Agencies and Entities executing programs  and projects 
(in the case of Balsas: CONAFOR) financed with external credit in terms of Preparation, Negotiation; Monitoring and compliance with contractual commitments; verification of procurement processes; training and technical 
assistance, Disbursements of eligible expenses; financial monitoring and debt servicing.  
124. Within HACIENDA, the International Financial Affairs Unit (UAIH, by its acronym in Spanish) is in charge 
of: i) formulating and undertaking the Public Finance policy in its relations with foreign countries, as well as 
negotiations with international financial agencies; ii) exercising the rights, and managing the fulfillment of the 
obligations with banks and international organizations; iii) advising on new project proposals linked to external 
credit and; iv) negotiating and signing grants and non- reimbursable technical cooperation agreements with 
foreign agencies and institutions, as well as coordinating and following up on them.  
125. The Treasury  of the Federation (TESOFE) is an administrative Unit of the HACIENDA in charge of the 
financial management of the Federal Government's resources and securities.  In the Balsas project, TESOFE 
transfers budgetary resources to CONAFOR, which advances the expenses of the Project activities funded by the IFAD -GCF Loans . As such, HACIENDA  will be an Executing Entity, but it will not implement any Funded 
Activity.  
126. As HACIENDA is the authorized Representative of the Borrower, it will sign th e Subsidiary Loan 
Agreement with IFAD. T he UAIH designated NAFIN as the financial agent and CONAFOR as the executing agent 
for the GCF L oans . As the proceeds  of the GCF Loan A and the GCF Loan B  will be disbursed by IFAD to the 
Financial Agent (NAFIN), which channels the funds to the Treasury of the Federation (TESOFE) within 
HACIENDA, and TESOFE subsequently channels it to CONAFOR, HACIENDA will be an Executing Entity, but it will not implement any Funded Activity.  
 
NAFIN (Executing Entity  2 – “EE2”) 
 
127. Nacional Financiera, S.N.C., Banca de Desarrollo (NAFIN) is an ac credited national Direct Access Entity 
(DAE)  based in Mexico. As mentioned above, NAFIN will act as the fiduciary of the l oans  of the Project and will 
channel the funds from IFAD to CONAFOR , as CONAFOR cannot directly receive external loan financing . As 
such,  NAFIN will be an Executing Entity, but it will not implement any Funded Activity.  NAFIN confirms the 
disbursement and ensures that the funds have been credited to its account. Subsequently, NAFIN auctions the 
funds in the money market, and once the auction is completed, it transfers the equivalent amount in pesos to the
