Scale  Currently, a transition to 
climate -resilient and 
low-emission production 
systems in the Balsas 
Project Area (PA) is not 
prioritized as forest 
development incentives 
invested via institutional 
modalities (e.g. 
PADFSB ,) is not based 
on climate change adaptation and Low The project will transform production 
systems and land use forms in the 
Balsas PA into systems which are 
climate resilient and low in GHG 
emissions, targeting the most climate 
vulnerable and poor rural communities in 
the country, specifically the most 
vulnerable municipalities and the areas of 
the PA with the highest levels of poverty 
and extreme poverty.  The project will promote the 
mainstreaming of the new climate- based 
approach into the main rural development institutions (and their incentive programs 
and initiatives) operating in the Balsas PA 
(CONAFOR, CONAGUA and AGRICULTURAAGRICULTURA ) 
fostering multi -sectoral coordination at 
Basin level. This will be achieved through: i.) operationalizing the use of climate change criteria in current 
institutional support modalities for the 
prioritization of integral territorial 
development through a community -

*Page 69*

E  
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 65 
  
  
 mitigation needs. 
Consequently, climate-
resilient and low -
emission development in the Basin PA is not 
scalable.  centric, bottom -up approach and; ii.) 
investing in the climate- sensitive 
management of ecosystems, and climate-
resilient productive systems and value 
chains for adaptation and mitigation 
benefits. As a result,  integral territorial 
development support in the PA will adopt 
a decentralized, coordinated and climate-responsive approach across all sectors, 
and presents a model that can be scaled 
up across Mexico’s rural areas.
 
Replicability  An explicit model for 
climate -resilient and 
low-emission 
development for production systems in 
the forestry and 
agriculture sectors in 
Mexico’s has not yet 
been proven. As such, 
there is no proof of 
concept for replication 
by multisectoral 
coordination structures. 
W  Low The project’s paradigm shift has the 
potential to be replicated across Mexico: i.) Through the operationalization of 
climate change criteria in the prioritization 
of rural development incentives and; ii.) 
through the strengthening of multisectoral 
coordination structures to re- align other 
sources of national development finance 
towards climate finance, such as those of 
CONAGUA. These approaches are the 
basis of the project’s financial exit 
strategy and its potential for replicability 
in Mexico.  Piloting of an incentivized climate-sensitive 
approach to integral territorial development 
will first take place within CONAFOR’s 
Reglas de Operación. In addition, 
specialized technical assistance will be 
provided by the project to facilitate access of those most poor and vulnerable 
communities that traditionally have had 
less or difficult access to CONAFOR’s 
support programs through a demand driven, par ticipatory approach. This will 
be complemented by the implementation of a pilot program to increase ease of access to PADFSB  support by the most 
vulnerable communities in the Basin. To ensure that the paradigm shift is achieved at a decentralized level and not top down, 
multisectoral platforms will be established  
at the sub- basin and ethnic territory level 
to foster the participation of local 
communities and all levels of 
government. Furthermore, the project will support existing and create new local 
facilitators. This local -level bottom- up 
approach will promote more flexible 
procedures for the channeling of 
development finance to climate change 
initiatives via Basin and Sub -Basin


---
*Page 70*
---

E  
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 66 
  
  
 Committees (SBCs). The piloting of such 
a cross -cutting approach by CONAFOR 
in the Balsas Basis provides a model that 
can be replicated across sectors in 
Mexico’s climate -vulnerable areas without 
further external financial contributions.  
Sustainability  There is currently no 
consistent long- term 
budget allocation to 
climate finance within 
Mexico’s development 
framework. Financial 
flows are mainly directly 
to traditional 
development projects in 
the country’s rural 
areas.  
Low The core medium-  to long- term result of 
the project is related to the application of 
the paradigm shifting innovations 
introduced above, first piloted in the 
Balsas PA using GCF concessional 
finance, that will have the potential to 
realign an average of USD 160 million 
per year of forest development finance 
provided by CONAFOR towards climate 
finance (for adaptation and mitigation) in 
Mexico’s most vulnerable communities.  
This amount of USD 160 million per year 
will result in the re -alignment of USD 1.5 
billion climate finance during the lifespan 
of the project (20 years). The continued 
flow of resources required for the 
projected USD 1.5 billion in leveraged 
climate finance, is guaranteed by the 
resources budgeted by the Government 
of Mexico for integral territorial 
development over the next 20 years.  The project's sustainability depends on 
transforming institutional modalities used 
to allocate incentives for forest 
development initiatives to include climate 
change criteria, re- aligning forestry and 
forest landscape finance as climate 
finance for adaptation and mitigation 
initiatives in the most vulnerable areas of 
the Balsas PA. The financial exit strategy 
is built into this paradigm- shifting 
approach. This will ensure that re- aligned 
climate finance flows into adaptation and 
mitigation initiatives in M exico are 
sustained in the medium to long term.  
The prioritization and selection of interventions and sites using climate 
change criteria will inform climate-
focused support in rural areas with the 
highest vulnerability, while the multi -
annual progress -based payments system 
will allow for the renewal of support in the 
form of incentives in these areas, 
increasing the scale and sustainability  
impacts of the climate- focused initiatives. 
Sustainability will also be supported by: i) 
Establishing participatory mechanisms for 
local and micro- regional planning and 
setting up social rules that afford 
territorial land- use management while 
taking into account the threats and 
opportunities linked to climate change; ii) 
Developing forestry, agroforestry and


---
*Page 71*
---

E  
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 67 
  
  
 adaptive forestry system technologies 
which promote native tree species and its 
spatial arrangements and combinations 
better adapted to climate change; and iii) 
Encouraging existing rural extension 
systems to incorporate a climate 
dimension into their tech nical assistance 
services and strengthening organizational 
and technology transfers, especially for 
the most vulnerable populations.

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

*Page 87*

 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 83 
  
  
 186. During project implementation, CONAFOR will be responsible for overall Project monitoring. The project will recruit a full ti me Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) 
expert who will collaborate with the UCP and ETP of CONAFOR. The Project’s progress will be meas ured against the indicators included in the Project Logical 
Framework above. A project -specific Monitoring and Evaluation plan will put together according to the approved Logical Framework. The plan includes a household 
baseline and at least two external evaluations during Project implementation (at midterm and final review), a permanent strategy to collect and analyze informati on 
related to the implementation of the Project. The final review will assess the achievement of indicators, sustainability of r esults, and lessons learned. The parameters 
of such evaluations will be outlined in the Operation Manual.  
187. Although impact indicators are essentially related to the improvement in the capacity in adopting new climate- resilient forest landscape management and 
livelihood strategies, the Logical Framework also includes substantial  GHG emission reduction benefits . At the impact, outcome, and output levels, the indicators 
disaggregate targets by gender, age, and ethnic composition of the beneficiaries. The M&E system will also measure improvement in terms of the development of new 
institutional instruments and mechan isms oriented to mainstream climate change in policies, operational rules of national programs, and coordination efforts among 
the different levels of the Government. The Logical Framework also includes indicators that reflect the progress of the Project i n terms of the forest landscape areas 
that are managed according to new climate change criteria and nature- based solutions. In addition, a set of deliverables for each activity is identified to enable the 
project team to follow up on the progress of the di fferent outcomes of the Project. Each Multiannual Invest Plan supported in the prioritized areas will also include basic 
indicators, whose progress will trigger the corresponding annual financial and technical supports.  
188. The collection of M&E data in the field and at the CONAFOR level will combine different systems and activities:  
o The Integrated Information System of Supports of CONAFOR  (“Sistema Integral de Información de Apoyos”  SIIAC for the Spanish acronym) collects 
and records information on the proposals formulated by potential beneficiaries, the evaluation of these proposals and the sup ported actions by the 
PADFSB in the rural communities, including the Multi - Annual Investment Plans. SIACC comprises a geospatial application that allows for the 
localization of the beneficiaries, the intervened polygons, and the delimitation of the rural communities. SIIAC will be a  major information system to 
collect, analyse, and report information related to the beneficiaries and actions supported by the project .  
o Regarding overlapping / double counting of beneficiaries:  To grant and incentive payment within the PADFSB, CONAFOR signs a legal agreement 
with the benefitting A grarian Community  or group. This legal agreement has to include a list of the heads of the household that are going to benefit 
from the supported activities, including their names and their personal identification number “Clave Única del Registro de Población”  (CURP). This 
information is registered within CONAFORs´ information system for PADFSB forest incentives, the SIICA , which allows CONAFOR for full traceability 
of the granted incentives. There are cases in which a household benefits from more than one activity, as some are synergistic  and/or complementary. 
In those cases , CONAFOR eliminates any possible double counting or duplication by using the unique personal identification number CURP, as its  
holder is co unted as only one beneficiary for the CONAFOR.  
o Household survey.  At the beginning and the final of the project, a household survey will be carried out to collect social, cultural, and econom ic 
information on a probabilistic sample of potential project beneficiaries, who will be later divided between control and treat ment groups. This survey 
aims to obtain quantitative and qualitative information to measure improvement in climate resilience, livelihood, and adoptio n of new practices, as well 
as gender and ethnic disaggregated information.  
o The project will follow the IFAD’s Guidelines to measure several corporative Core Indicators (CI) that were incorporated in t he Balsas logical framework. 
The CIs consist of 1 outreach, 20 output and 22 outcome indicators thatare mapped to the strategic obj ectives (SOs) and areas of thematic focus of 
IFAD Strategic Framework 2016- 2025; they are also aligned with the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) defined in the 2030 Agenda.  
o The Local Brigades for Measuring, Reporting and Verification (MRV)  is a new CONAFOR initiative that will be piloted through the Balsas project and 
then scaled up at the national level. Within the project area, 18 brigades formed by one local expert and two rural youth (men and women) will be 
recruited by CONAFOR to parti cipate in the data collection for the National Forest Inventory, the estimation of the deforestation rate, as well as the


---
*Page 88*
---

E  
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 84 
  
  
 monitoring of a sample of plots where the Project supports the improvement of the management of forest landscapes. These MRV Brigades will report 
data to the CONAFOR MRV team through a new online application that will be developed by the Project (refer to Activity 3.1.3).  
o The Project will also recruit independent consultants  who will assess project progress in terms of main impacts, outcomes and output achievements 
and will verify whether the M&E system reports the targets properly. These consultants will also participate in field visits to organize focus group 
discussions an d evaluate the level of satisfaction of the beneficiaries, as well as correct safeguards implementation.

 F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT  
F.1.  Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)  
Selected Risk Factor 1   
Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and operational  Low Medium  
Description  
Low annual project budget allocations and/or the failure to allocate resources in accordance with the project timeline 
can seriously disrupt disbursements and operations.  
 
Mitigation Measure(s)  
IFAD’s experience in Mexico has shown national systems to be advanced and reliable, which will ensure that the 
probability of this risk occurring remains low. To further mitigate this risk and ensure that project implementation is not 
adversely affected, I FAD as AE will work closely with CONAFOR (EE) to promptly allocate sufficient budgetary 
resources over the project life cycle.   
Selected Risk Factor 2   
Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and operational  Medium  Medium  
Description  
Lack of coordination between the various state agencies involved in project coordination (including CONAFOR, AGRICULTURA , SEMARNAT, BIENESTAR, FIRA and INPI amongst others). This is linked to a lack of platforms to 
foster dialogue.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
The project will contribute to building the institutional capacities of these stakeholders by strengthening inter -
institutional coordination at the federal, state and municipal level. In addition, the project will ensure regular 
engagements take place with the institutional stakeholders and ensure that platforms are in place to bring them 
together for dialogue and planning related to project implementation. The risk will also be mitigated through close 
coordination with and support from the Office for the C oordination of Advisers to the Presidency of the Republic, 
HACIENDA  and the implementing partners.  
Selected Risk Factor 3   
Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and operational  Medium  Medium  
Description  
Economic crises may arise as a result of the disasters such as the COVID -19 pandemic. This, in turn, could lead to 
reduced government revenue and thus, limited public investment in initiatives and incentives for rural production or 
forest landscape conserv ation activities, including PES.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
In the case of unavoidable disasters, such as the COVID -19 pandemic, which may result in economic crises, 
investment in rural production and forest landscape conservation activities will be safeguarded through predetermined integral territorial budgets all ocated in advance by CONAFOR. CONAFOR’s integral territorial budgets 
are purposed to support and protect economic development in rural areas, including during economic crises (such as that caused by the COVID -19 pandemic), which will contribute to mitigati ng this risk .  
Selected Risk Factor 4   
Category  Probability  Impact


---
*Page 90*
---

E   GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 86 
   
 Forex  Medium  High 
Description  
Fluctuations in the exchange range, particularly devaluation of the Mexican peso relative to the US dollar, negatively 
impact project budget planning, disbursement and spending, which may disrupt the implementation of the project.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
The GCF funding will be disbursed quarterly throughout the project’s term, in the historically and presumptively more 
stable currency of the US Dollar, which will mitigate the impact of any devaluation of the Mexican peso relative to the dollar. In its Co- financing agreements, IFAD expects to require CONAFOR to hold this Co- financing in Mexican peso 
denominated accounts to allow for an onshore account. Although the project’s financial model is articulated in US Dollars, most costs will eventually be incurred in Mexican pesos, with currency conversion to take place upon receipt 
by CONAFOR for project costs. The Operating Manual governing CONAFOR’s management of these funds 
incorporates a strategy to mitigate exchange rate risk. The project financial model als o includes a contingency fund 
that may be available to mitigate the impact of currency fluctuation.  
Selected Risk Factor 5   
Category  Probability  Impact  
Other  Low Medium  
Description  
The project may face a change in the Federal Government, shifting priorities in the target region and resulting in 
varied institutional support during the implementation period.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
This risk will be mitigated by aligning the project with state programmes aimed at climate change mitigation and 
poverty reduction — both during project development and implementation. This will ensure that the project is tightly 
aligned with government pr iorities at all times during the implementation period. Similarly, the project will align with 
other platforms and public contexts which may foster opportunities for relevant dialogue with stakeholder, to ensure 
continued support.  
Selected Risk Factor 6   
Category  Probability  Impact  
Other  Medium  High 
Description  
Forest fires (caused by humans) and extreme weather events (such as floods) may result in damage to or loss of forest cover, negatively affecting the progress and success of reforestation activities.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
Selected Risk Factor 7 
Category  Probability  Impact  
Other  Mediu m Medium  
Description  
Organized crime in the Project Region may hinder or delay project implementation and/or put project personnel at 
risk. 
Mitigation Measure(s)  
. 
Military intelligence of the Government of Mexico informs the CONAFOR, as well as all other government institutions, 
about the presence and the situation relative of organized crime in their respective region. CONAFOR´s local field 
offices (“promotorías) obtain dir ect information about the security situation so that dangerous situations can be 
avoided and the security of CONAFOR´s technical staff is granted. The project will reinforce the communication

 between CONAFOR headquarters and the promotorías through the establishment of the project UCP , the recruitment 
of additional facilitators in the field, (which will have to adhere to the security protocols), equipment (including 
communication) and intensive training of the technical team, particularly advanced security in the field training.

 G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS  
G.1.  Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)  
189. To identify environmental and social risks and propose appropriate mitigation measures, an Environmental 
and Social Management Framework (ESMF) has been prepared for the project (Annex 6). Based on the assessment 
conducted, the project is categorized as a Category B or ‘moderate risk’ according to the GCF project classification. This categorization was selected because any potential environmental and social impacts related to activities (especially under Outcome 2) will be localized and easily reversible through already known and commonly used 
mitigation measures. All sub- projects under Outcome  2 will be screened to ensure a Category B risk level or lower 
and abbreviated Environmental and Social Management Plans (ESMPs) to screen and monitor eventual impacts will 
be prepared.  
190. CONAFOR has an adequate institutional framework to assess and manage potential social and 
environmental impacts. CONAFOR´s operational rules ( Reglas de Operación - ROs) and manuals ( guías ) are used to 
screen and monitor community project proposals and prevent the funding of any socially or environmentally harmful activities as per Mexican Law and safeguard requirements. The project will strengthen these ROs and CONAFOR´s 
capacities. CONAFOR has an extensive experience in safeguards gained by working on the design and 
implementation of diverse programs and activities with IFAD, the World Bank, the Forest Investment Program, the Bio Carbon Fund, and bilateral cooperation with Norway and others. Its safeguard institutional fr amework is also aligned 
with the GCF´s safeguards requirements. CONAFOR has successfully managed loans of over USD 500 million and 
grants of approximately USD 25 million without any significant safeguard issues.  
191. The project seeks to increase the resilience of vulnerable forest -dependent communities and the ecosystems 
they depend on. In terms of Indigenous Peoples of the Tlapaneco, Zapoteco, Amuzgo, Mixteco, Chatino, Tacuate, 
Triqui, Nahua and Purépecha ethnic groups  and other vulnerable groups, the greatest risk identified is the possibility 
that their positive and active role in climate action is not adequately recognized by focusing narrowly on them as mere 
beneficiaries rather than on their potential leadership.  In this sense, the most significant risks revolve around:  
● Inadequate indigenous and vulnerable groups’ participation.  
● Marginalization and erosion of Indigenous and vulnerable groups’ aspirations, capacities, knowledge, 
practices, and perspectives; specifically:  
o Indigenous planning practices  
o Indigenous knowledge around land stewardship  
o Indigenous governance practices  
o Indigenous economies of reciprocity  
192. The project´s environmental risks and impacts on biodiversity and natural habitats are very small, introduction 
of invasive species, use of forbidden pesticides, undue use of natural resources are prevented at the inception phase of sub- project through the use of CONAFOR´s screening tools and exclusion list. To ensure proper implementation of 
these restrictions project extension agents will receive additional capacity building for safeguards implementation and monitoring.  
193. A Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) to handle eventual questions, complaints, suggestions, access to 
information requests, comments and grievances is already in place at CONAFOR (called the Mecanismo de Acción 
Ciudadana, MAC). The operation of the MAC is carried out in three different areas that have their own channels, 
regulations, and attention protocols, depending on the nature of the matters to resolve and/or attend, namely: (i) Internal Control Body (includes the Social Comptrollers); (ii) Liaison Unit  - INAI (National Institute for Access to 
Information); and (iii) Citizen Information and Attention Service (SIAC). If any project -related grievances cannot be 
resolved by CONAFOR’s MAC, they will be elevated to the level of IFAD’s GRM/Complaints procedure. IFAD 
Complaints Procedure exists to ensure that appropriate mechanisms are in place to allow individuals and 
communities to contact IFAD directly and file a complaint if they believe they are or may be adversely affected by the 
project not complying with IFAD’s social and environmental policies. Complainants  are thus encouraged to first bring 
the matter to the attention of CONAFOR as the EE but may bring the issue directly to IFAD if they feel the EE has not 
adequately responded or if they feel they might be subject to retaliation if they approach the EE directly.  
194. Coordination of safeguards monitoring will be the responsibility of the Gerencia de Biodiversidad y Cambio 
Climático  (GBCC). In order to complement GCBB supervision capacity at the field level, safeguards monitoring will 
also be undertaken by the Promotorías Forestales (PF)  and its staff, who will receive capacity building for this 
purpose. The GBCC will be in charge of overseeing training and capacity building at the PF to ensure proper safeguards implementation and monitoring. The PF will monitor safeguar ds implementation at the field  level ensuring 
compliance with safeguard requirements.  
195. Relevant documentation related to safeguards will be disclosed in a timely manner. For Category B projects, 
disclosure shall be at least 30 days before the expected date of approval of the GCF in accordance with the 
requirements of the GCF Disclosure Polic y. Sub -projects at the state level will disclose all relevant documentation at 
least 30 days before the expected date of approval by CONAFOR. Documents disclosed will be presented in an


---
*Page 93*
---

G   GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 89 
   
 accessible and culturally appropriate manner, accounting for the specific needs of Indigenous and community groups 
that may be affected by the implementation of the project (such as literacy, gender, language differences, 
accessibility of technical information or connectivity).  
196. During the consultation process with the representatives of the Agrarian Communities (ACs) of the prioritized 
indigenous municipalities of the Purépecha, Amuzgo, Tlapaneco, Mixtec  and Nahua  peoples, emphasis was placed 
on mechanisms and forms of resolution of possible conflicts generated from the implementation of the project to 
safeguard the communities. The participants highlighted the importance of the community assembly as the highest 
institution for resolving internal conflicts and decision- making, as well as the importance of ensuring the participation 
of institutions such as CONAFOR in the community assemblies to clearly inform communities about the objectives and activities of the project. To do so, a complete set of activities, including a free and prior informed consultation 
(FPIC) plan, has been developed (see Annex 7a) to ensure Indigenous communities’ participation in project 
development and to prevent any potential conflict during implementation.  
G.2.  Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
197. In line with GCF's gender policies, the project stimulates climate investments that contribute to reducing 
gender gaps and promotes equality through climate change mitigation and adaptation measures. To this end, a Gender Assessment and Action Plan (GAP) (please see also Annex 8) was carried out that shows, that in the 
Mexican countryside and specifically in the project area women are more affected by poverty than men, as 56% of 
women are poor
99. Women represent only 26% of landowners and gender stereotypes and machismo  are 
determining factors in their living conditions and their possibilities for economic and social empowerment. Among the most relevant gender gaps are the long working hours, the invisibility of their contributions to production, conservation of the envi ronment, nutrition, and the family economy. Women have higher levels of illiteracy
100, are less connected 
than men to the mass media101 and hold much fewer102 decision- making positions in ejidos  and communities. Only 
one third of women in agriculture are paid for their work and have less access to credit and technical assistance.  
198. Women are more vulnerable to the effects of climate change. Household activities become more burdensome 
due to rising temperatures and scarcity (or excess) of water, increasing the difficulties of washing, cleaning, and 
cooking. The occurrence of pathogeni c diseases and the limited availability of food for the family also add to women's 
psychological and work burdens. Traspatio  subsistence production and economic activities such as harvesting of 
non-forest timber products, which are also often carried out by women, are affected by the loss of vegetation. More 
women are the heads of households due to the labour migration of men and are in charge of agricultural production, 
but they lack  recognition of their labour and land ownership. Inequity in access to services and goods for production 
and resilience to climate change, affect women's performance and increase their vulnerability.  
199. Based on these findings, recommendations were formulated in the GAP to ensure women's participation in 
project activities and benefits by defining quotas or participation targets, capacity building for gender equality for the 
different groups of project st akeholders. The GAP describes existing national laws and policies, strategies and plans 
of the institutions involved in the project. The GAP is elaborated based on the project's expected results and outputs 
framework and defines activities, indicators, and targets for women's participation. Through its implementation, 
women are expected to be active participants and beneficiaries of increased social and climate resilience, improved climate adaptation of productive ecosystems and rural community development.  Furthermore the GAP:  
● convenes and strengthens women's participation in decision- making in RCIMTs for the prioritization of 
intervention areas as well as in the elaboration of participatory multi -annual investment plans  
● promotes greater gender equity in institutional work by adjusting sectoral and/or institutional policies, strategies, and regulations  
● includes women as beneficiaries of investment plans in basins and micro- basins and for this purpose 
develops communication and awareness campaigns to disseminate calls for projects with inclusive language 
and based on profiles of the target population according to gender  
● prepares simplified formats for the submission of project proposals  
● proposes training in gender and inclusion for extensionists, local facilitators, brigadistas  and promotores 
forestales , and it´s up- take into CONAFOR's capacity building programme  
● proposes to prioritize the participation of women in ejido  and communal authorities and to use selection 
criteria for Payment of Environmental Services (PES) projects that favours women's participation  
● promotes that women participate in projects to improve the productive systems of the farming communities, be trained in production practices and technologies adapted to climate change and variability, and benefit 
from the financial transfers made by the pr oject to AC´s for the hiring of technical assistance, provision of 
inputs and compensation of labour costs  
● proposes that climate- resilient sustainable forest management activities open- up opportunities for women's 
participation in value chains, especially in economic activities linked to sustainable use and value addition to timber and non- timber forest product s, strengthening their capacities on gender, sustainable and climate-
resilient production practices, organizational, managerial, and marketing strengthening


---
*Page 94*
---

G   GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 90 
   
 ● proposes to implement comprehensive productive projects for women that include affirmative actions with a 
gender perspective and support backyard food production through productive diversification and improved 
irrigation  
● proposes to improve women's access to climate information and grant its equitable use, including training in 
the use of smartphones  
● proposes to carry out workshops for the exchange of experiences and good practices implemented by women in the framework of the project  
200. The GAP includes indicators and targets for monitoring and evaluation. It also provides for the availability of 
specialized gender support for the implementation of the Plan and gender mainstreaming throughout the project.  
G.3.  Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
201. The project's financial management (FM) will be carried out in accordance with the legal framework 
governing public administration in Mexico. FM will be the responsibility of the International Affairs and Financial 
Promotion Unit (UAIFF) of CONAFOR; the UCP will contribute to FM and will have a Finance specialist who will work 
closely with the UAIFF.  
202. NAFIN, as the Financial Agent designated by the Ministry of Finance, will be responsible for the 
administration of the financing. It will follow -up on audits, managing disbursements, and will be in charge of loan 
disbursement processes that the GCF will channel through IFAD via NAFIN and TESOFE to CONAFOR.  
203. CONAFOR with its Fondo Forestal Mexicano will follow -up on audits, managing disbursements, and will be in 
charge of grant disbursement processes that the GCF will channel through IFAD to CONAFOR.  
204. For disbursements  of GCF Loan A and GCF Loan B , the reimbursement method will be used given that 
project expenses are pre- financed by the Government. For grants, a replenishment mechanism could be used based 
on an advance payment. The bank accounts will be those defined by NAFIN, from which funds are transferred to the 
Treasury of the Federation (TESOFE) in the case of the loans , and to CONAFOR in the case of the grant. The flow of 
funds to beneficiaries is controlled by CONAFOR Integrated Support System (S IIAC) and by Integral administration 
System (SAI) for other expenses. IFAD's Client Portal will be the online platform used by NAFIN and CONAFOR  to 
submit disbursement requests of the GCF Loan A, GCF Loan B and GCF  grant proceeds, respectively,  to IFAD.  
205. The internal audit will be carried out by the Internal Control Organ (OIC) of CONAFOR, and the external audit 
will be carried out by private firms to be appointed by the SFP applying the terms of reference agreed with the WB 
and IDB, accepted by IFAD. IFAD's responsibility will consist of on- site supervision missions to assess FM 
arrangements, ident ify areas requiring improvement, fiduciary risks, and corresponding mitigation measures.  
206. Procurement activities will be carried out in accordance with Mexico's national procurement system. The 
procurement methods will be those established in those regulations and the maximum thresholds per method will be 
those established for each fiscal year in the Federal Expenditure Budget (PEF). IFAD will maintain the review prior to 
the processes based on the thresholds resulting from the application of the Procurement Risk Matrix (PRM).  A risk 
assessment was undertaken in August 2021 during the design and it concluded that Mexico’s procurement rules are 
aligned with international standards. This was confirmed in the last supervision mission for the IFAD loan to Mexico in 
2023.  
207. Procurement planning will be done through IFAD's current model procurement plan (PP), which will indicate, 
among others, the selection methods, and the type of review by IFAD (prior or post -review) to which the processes 
will be subject. The first PP will be for the first year of operation of the project and subsequent PPs will be annual.  
The PIM includes  the need of providing an 18- months PP in the first year of the project.  
208. Requests for non- objection, both to the PP and its modifications, as well as to the procurement processes 
established in the PP as subject to prior review, will be processed through the No Objection Tracking Utility System 
(NOTUS). Contracts arising from procurements raised in the PP will be registered in IFAD´s Client Portal (ICP) 
Contract Management Tool (CMT) platform. Procurement management will be reviewed and evaluated by IFAD 
during supervision missions which will be carried out periodically, but at least twice a year.

entity is providing to the Secretariat the follow ing two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:  
● full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with 
an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the 
accredited entity’s disclosure policy,  and

 ● redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.

Secretariat’s assessment  of FP236 
Proposal name:  Basin Approach for Livelihood Sustainability through Adaptation 
Strategies (BALSAS)  
Accredited entity:  International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD)  
Country:  Mexico  
Project/programme size : Medium  
I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat  
1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following 
remarks:  
Strengths  Points of caution  
This project i ntroduc es a new integrated 
territorial development approach by using  
climate  change  related criteria for selecting 
and prioritizing the most vulnerable beneficiaries, interventions and sites.  The project has the potential to unlock up to 
USD 1.5 billion in domestic public finance by aligning federal rural development subsidies with climate priorities. However, a chieving 
the USD 1.5 billion  figure depends on the 
federal government scaling up the new 
approach to allocation of rural development 
funds, which lies outside the scope of the 
project.
 
The BALSAS project’s s ustainability will be  
increased by using and strengthen ing 
existing  public domestic rural extension and 
development channels.   
Its p otential leverage ratio is 1:40 thanks to 
the possibility of scaling  up the realignment 
of domestic public finance flows with climate objectives  (USD 1.5 billion  over 20 years ).  
The project f ocus es on one of Mexico’s 
poorest and most vulnerable regions, the population of which includ es Indigenous 
Peoples.   
II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment  
2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the 
term sheet agreed between the Secretariat and the accredited entity, and, if considered 
appropriate, subject to the conditions set out in Annex II of document GCF/B.39/02.  
2.1 Project  background


---
*Page 105*
---

 
GCF/B.3 9/02/Add. 11 
Page 105 
 
 
3. The Balsas Basin in Mexico, a region highly vulnerable to climate change, faces 
exacerbated challenges  as one of the country’s poorest and most marginalized areas . Climate 
effects such as increased temperatures, erratic rainfall,  and extended dry seasons are reducing 
agricultural productivity and degrading natural resources. These changes lead to more frequent 
flooding, crop damage and droughts, further reducing crop yields. Local efforts to combat these 
issues are hindered by ina dequate capacities and inst itutional frameworks that fail to prioritize 
climate adaptation in vulnerable areas.  
4. The cross -cutting project ( 78 per cent adaptation, 22 per cent mitigation) aims to 
increase the climate resilience of the most vulnerable communities and ecosystems, and to 
reduce emissions from production systems in the BALSAS project area by:   
(a) Operationalizing the use of climate- change criteria in current institutional modalities for 
the prioritization of rural development incentives through a community -centric bottom -
up approach;  
(b) Investing in the climate -sensitive management of ecosystems, and climate -resilient 
productive systems and value chains for adaptation and mitigation benefits; and  
(c) Supporting a climate information and risk management system, and the systematic 
management of knowledge and lessons learn ed. 
5. The cost for the proposed project is estimated at USD 90,953,000 , of which GCF 
investment  is requested of USD 20,000,000 in loans and USD 19,500,000 in grant s.  
6. The project is expected to achieve a mitigation outcome of 3,926,876 tonnes of carbon 
dioxide e quivalent ( t CO 2 eq) over 20 years and to increase the climate resilience of 3,309,211  
people , including 201,867  (6.5 per cent of the of the population of prioritized watersheds and 
Indigenous territories)  as direct beneficiaries.  
7. GCF rates t he program me as category B (medium) for environmental and social 
safeguards .  
2.2 Component -by-component analysis  
8. The project is split into three  components leading to the following outcomes : 
(a) Outcome  1: Government program mes and community development plans integrate 
climate -change criteria for the allocation of support ; 
(b) Outcome  2: Forest landscapes are sustainably managed and better adapted to climate 
change for the provision of environmental services and increased livelihood resilience ; 
and 
(c) Outcome  3: The producers, inhabitants and authorities of the B ALSAS  project area 
receive  timely and relevant climate information for climate risk management, disaster 
prevention,  and knowledge management . 
 
Component 1:  Operationalization of a system that prioritizes sites, beneficiaries and interventions 
for rural development support in the Balsas Basin using climate- change criteria ( Total cost: USD 
10,091,726; GCF cost: USD 2,492, 891 in loans and USD 6,128,047 in grants ) 
9. The outcome of component 1 is that government program mes and community 
development plans integrate climate- change criteria for the allocation of support .  
10. The activities of c omponent 1 are :  
(a) Activity 1.1.1. Facilitate the creation of forest management units and r egional 
committees for the i ntegrated management of the t erritory ;


---
*Page 106*
---

 
GCF/B.3 9/02/Add. 11 
Page 106 
 
 
(b) Activity 1.1.2 . Agree on priority intervention areas at the micro -basin level ;  
(c) Activity 1.1.3. Strengthen multisectoral coordination structures, operation rules , and a 
mechanism for co -investment within the basins;   
(d) Activity 1.1.4. Strengthen and operationalize criteria for adaptation to climate change in 
institutional program me procedures ;  
(e) Activity 1.2.1 . Create a c ommunication and awareness campaign to disseminate calls for 
proposals ;  
(f) Activity 1.2.2 . Formulat e climate -smart multiannual investment plan s based on micro -
basin zoning plans and validated through free , prior and informed consent ;  
(g) Activity 1.3.1 . Implement  capacity development program mes for extension workers, 
local facilitators and “brigades” ;  
(h) Activity 1.3.2 . Support and creat e forest protection brigades and local climate change 
resilience facilitators;  and  
(i) Activity 1.3.3: Strengthen the Promotorías forestales  (regional and local offices)  of 
CONAFOR (the National Forestry Commission of Mexico) .  
Component 2 : Climate change prioritization criteria applied to the allocation of rural development 
incentives for the climate -sensitive management of ecosystems, and climate -resilient systems and 
value chains  (USD 73 ,496,484; GCF cost: USD 17,507,109 in loans and USD 9,462,362 in grants )

better adapted to climate change.

strategic areas ;

III. Assessment of performance against investment  criteria   
16. The programme scores well against GCF investment criteria . A detailed  assessment of  
the programme’s fit with GCF investment criteria is provided below .  
3.1 Impact potential                                                            Scale: Medium  to high  
17. The impact potential is expected to  be medium to high , as the number of direct 
beneficiaries total  201 ,867 (members of agrarian communities, small producers, and 
Indigenous groups) , including  40 per cent of women . Likewise, a large mitigation impact with 
3,926 ,876 Mt CO 2 eq over 20 years will be achieved  from the reduced degradation and therefore 
enhanced conservation of 324,578  hectares  of forests.  
18. Direct beneficiaries of the project are defined as small producers of agrarian 
communities from 121 of the most socially  and climate vulnerable municipalities in the BALSAS  
project area , focusing on I ndigenous communities (82 of the 121 municipalities).  These direct 
beneficiaries are assessed with a focus on those impacted by land use cover change and value 
chain investments in component 2, as well as those benefiting from capacity -building and 
brigades across all components.  
19. Indirect beneficiaries of the project , totalling 3,107,344 individuals, are defined as 
members of the households of the direct beneficiaries, as well as other community members 
from the 68 agrarian communities targeted . Indirect beneficiaries were primarily estimated as a 
proportion of the inhabitants residing in the prioritized watersheds, where investments in 
component 2 are scheduled  to occur. Beneficiaries of early warning systems (component 3) 
correspond to the population of high -priority municipalities. Howeve r, as these municipalities 
largely overlap with the priority watersheds, they are not double counted in the total of indirect 
beneficiaries, reflecting a conservative approach.  
20. While primarily adaptation focused ( 78 per cent), the cross -cutting project also includes 
a mitigation component ( 22 per cent). The project will contribute to Mexico’s  2030 national 
target of reducing greenhouse gas ( GHG)  emissions by 22  per cent by implementing 
afforestation, reforestation and forest, farmland, and pasture management  (land use, land -use 
change and forestry sector) ; and agricultural soil management activities and programmed soil 
burns  (agriculture sector) .  
3.2 Paradigm  shift potential                                                         Scale: High 
21. The program me has the potential to be scaled up by enhancing public finance flows 
aligned with climate objectives and establishing a sustainable environment for institutional coordination, knowledge and data collection, and skill development. It is estimated that the


---
*Page 108*
---

 
GCF/B.3 9/02/Add. 11 
Page 108 
 
 
average of USD 160 million per year in traditional forestry finance provided by CONAFOR  – 
tota lling more than USD  1.54 billion (net present value) over the project ’s 20 -year lifespan  – will 
be redirected towards climate change adaptation and mitigation finance to benefit Mexico’s 
most vulnerable communities.  
22. The project  also has high potential for replication  across the country  through the 
operationalization of climate change criteria in the prioritization of rural development 
incentives and  by strengthening of multisectoral coordination structures to re -align other 
sources of national development finance towards climate finance,  as mentioned above . 
3.3 Sustainable development potential                                             Scale: High  
23. The programme  contributes to key Sustainable Development Goals (SDG s): SDG  13 
(Climate action ), SDG 1 (No poverty ); SDG 2 ( Zero hunger ); SDG 3 ( Good health and well -being ); 
SDG 5 ( Gender equality ); SDG 6 ( Clean water and sanitation ); and SDG 15 ( Life on land ). 
24. The major environmental  co-benefit is  the retention, regulation and release of rainwater 
flows within the ecosystems and the landscapes as both prolonged dr ought and more severe 
and heavy rainfalls pose important hazards for local communities and especially for the most 
vulnerable rural livelihoods that depend on forest products and agriculture for which a 
regulated water flow and soil humidity is vital.  
25. Another main  environmental co -benefit  is to promote the conservation of biodiversity 
and forest resources, resulting in increased availab ility  of habitat for wildlife, the preservation 
of culturally and aesthetically important areas, and improved ecosystem service supplies, such as disease regulation.  
3.4  Needs of the recipient                                                  Scale: Medium  to high 
26. The population of 15.8 million people living in the BALSAS  project area is extremely 
vulnerable to climate -change impacts due to considerably high er poverty rates and low er social 
development levels compared to the rest of Mexico. Inequities affecting women ’s access to 
services and assets have worsened  under climate -change effects. Additionally, about 55  per cent  
of the population, mainly young people, lack economic opportunities and community decision -
making involvement, reducing their resilience to climate change. Moreover, over 9.6 million Indigenous People living in poverty in the municipalities are particularly vulnerable.  
27. Though the Government of Mexico continue s to invest in the country’s forestry sector , 
there are several obstacles preventing the flow of tailored funding for climate change adaptation 
and mitigation in the project area , such as not specifically focus ing on the most vulnerable 
communities and ecosystems, limited access to credit for promoting the inclusion of small 
producers , and lack of inclusion of women and youth in ecosystem management and value chain 
activities.  
28. The project provides  financial and technical support to these vulnerable areas, and 
incorporates community needs through participatory planning . 
3.5 Country ownership                                                                       Scale: High 
29. This project  is highlighted as the top priority in Mexico’s pipeline by its national 
designated authority , the Mexican Ministry of Finance and Public Credit (Secretaría de Hacienda 
y Crédito Público , referred to as HACIENDA ), and has been developed in collaboration with 
multiple institutions and stakeholders under the lead of CONAFOR and the guidance of the


---
*Page 109*

GCF/B.3 9/02/Add. 11 
Page 109 
 
 
national designated authority . It is listed as one of the 100 promises of the favo urite candidate 
in the upcoming p residential elections.  
30. A no-objection letter has been issued by the  countr y. 
31. The project will aim  to use  and strengthen existing rural extension and development 
channels . 
3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness                                                            Scale:  High 
32. The estimated total cost of the project is USD  91 million, of which USD 73.5  million will 
be used in investment activities in c omponent 2.  
33. The potential l everage ratio is  1:40 thr ough  mobilization of USD 1.5 billion  over 20 years 
if scaled up across Mexico . 
34. The economic and financial analysis shows that this project is economically viable as a 
whole. The m ain sources of quantifiable benefits result from the investments in c omponent 2 
and include the increase in productivity, resilience of the productive  systems to climate change, 
and mitigation benefits from GHG emission reductions.   
35. The economic results at the aggregate level of the project are estimated based on the 
number of agrarian communities  and producer groups to be covered by the project . This step 
also includes the benefits related to climate- change mitigation through the impact on GHG 
emission reduction s estimated with the EX -Ante Carbon Balance Tool ( EX-ACT) for the different 
models . 
36. With  various scenarios of social carbon prices and discount rate , the economic internal 
rate of return of the project reaches a value between 16.6  per cent and 34.6  per cent and a 
positive economic  net present value  between USD 25  million and USD 216  million .  
IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies  
4.1 Environmental and social safeguards  
37. Project background.  The project aims to increase the climate resilience of the most 
vulnerable communities and ecosystems and  reduce emissions from production systems in the 
BALSAS  project area by enabling community- level investment in adaptation and mitigation 
initiatives, through the climate -focused transformation of rural development incentives for the 
conservation of forest resources and the strengthening of local institutions and capacit ies.  
38. Environmental and social (E&S) risk category and safeguards instrument.  The 
accredited entity (AE) has assigned an E&S risk category B rating to the project , given that the 
project will have potential limited adverse environmental and social impacts that are site 
specific and can easily be mitigated. This also takes account of the fact that the project’s 
activities are focused on the protection, conservation and restoration of natural resources and 
promote models of sustainable use of resources, such as wat er, forests and their timber and 
non- timber produc ts. The project will not be involved in actions and investments for the 
development of aquaculture or fishing, either on a medium or large scale, in ecologically 
sensitive areas and will not encourage the conversion of wetlands, changes in hydrology or the 
introduction of exotic marine species.  The Secretariat concurs with the categorization and 
confirms that it is within the E&S risk category that the AE is accredited for . The environmental 
and social co -benefits include conservation of biodiversity and improved access by the 
communities to environmental services. An e nvironmental and social management framework 
(ESMF) has been prepared , which indicates the processes and guidelines for the preparation,


---
*Page 110*
---

 
GCF/B.3 9/02/Add. 11 
Page 110 
 
 
implementation and supervision of E&S risk instruments designed for the project and its 
subprojects.  
39. Compliance with GCF’s Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) Standards.  The 
following paragraphs describe how the project complies with the standards.  
40. ESS1 : Assessment and management of e nvironmental and s ocial risks and 
impacts.  The environmental and social risks and impacts under the project are expected to be 
of moderate scale and can be readily managed and mitigated through the deployment of 
appropriate measures. Given the nature of the interventions , which are mostly related to 
capacity -building, environmental restoration , and targeted agricultural/agroforestry activities, 
there are no significant or cumulative impacts expected on biodiversity, natural habitats, land or 
water contamina tion , or on labo ur and working conditions. The ESMF follows the management 
procedures for identification, mitigation or compensation for any possible adverse 
environmental and social impacts derived from the execution of the B ALSAS  project. The ESMF 
will be used as guidance in examining the risks and impacts of the project and its subprojects 
when establishing the details of the exact locations and the potentially affected peoples. The 
ESMF establishes the principles, rules, guidelines and procedures for assessing  the E&S risk and 
impacts of the interventions during implementation.  
41. ESS2 : Labou r and w orking conditions.  The activities of the project will mostly be 
implemented on the ground by the local inhabitants of the ejidos  and rural and I ndigenous 
communities that are present within the locality. It is expected that the labo ur of migrants or 
temporary workers will not be required. Some of the low -risk activities in the forest areas 
include occupational health and safety risks , such as heat stress and dehydration due to 
exposure to high temperatures, accidents involving forest machiner y and exposure to biological 
hazards (e.g. allergic reactions to plants, pollen and insect bites and contracting vector -borne 
diseases ). The project will provide occupational health and safety training and promote the use 
of safety equipment when  conduct ing the activities. The project will ensure that international 
and national labo ur regulations, including the AE and GCF requirements , will be applied by 
those engaged by the project , including its beneficiaries.  
42. ESS3 : Resource efficiency and p ollution prevention.  The project intends to promote 
measures to reduce pollution and increase the sustainable use of resources , given that the 
project is focused on sustainable use of forest and water resources. Nevertheless, the activities 
could lead to generation of liquid and solid waste as a result of agricultural production and 
processing activities (e.g. crop residue, animal  manure, agrochemical containers, harvesting and 
processing waste, packaging material) that could pollute land and water resources. Land and water co ntamination may also occur from the use of fuels and lubricant s in machinery or the 
use of pesticides and/or agrochemicals. To mitigate these  impacts, the project will support the 
implementation of agroecological best practices such as composting, mulching, recycling 
packaging materials and ensur ing that the machiner y is properly maintained and serviced. 
Farmers will also be trained on the safe use and handling of agrochemicals. The project will also 
promote measures to reduce water use,  such as drop irriga tion and rainwater harvesting which 
could enhance infiltration, reduce soil erosion, improve water quality and minimize silting of 
water bodies.  
43. ESS4 : Community health, safety and s ecurity.  The AE has conducted consultations at 
the design stage , and the risk of conflicts among the targeted communities on the use of natural 
resources during implementation is expected to be minimized through the implementation of 
the free , prior and informed consent  plan and by ensuring that meaningful and continuous 
consultation and engagement with project stakeholders is implemented. The AE has identified the risk of the presence of organized crime in the project re gion , which may affect project 
implementation and/or put project personnel at risk. The project plans to mitigate this by 
recruiting additional facilitators in the field who will be made aware of security protocols and


---
*Page 111*
---

 
GCF/B.3 9/02/Add. 11 
Page 111 
 
 
provided with proper communications equipment and advanced and intensive field security 
training.  
44. ESS5 : Land acquisition and i nvoluntary resettlement.  The project will exclude any 
activity requiring land acquisition. The e xclusion list also includes “Activities that imply the 
economic displacement or physical resettlement of people” . The project will also ensure that 
“there is no risk of potential restriction of traditional access to land/livelihoods due to project 
activities”.   
45. ESS6 : Biodiversity conservation and s ustainable management of l iving natural 
resources.  The project seeks to improve the provision of ecosystem services, such as water 
capture, soil conservation and carbon sequestration. While it intends to conduct environmental 
restoration and promote biodiversity, some activities have the risk of introducing invasive 
species. As a mitigation measure, the ESMF states that “No invasive species are allowed on 
CONAFOR supported projects. Unwarranted  introduction will lead to the suppression of 
invasive species individuals”.   
46. ESS7 : Indigenous Peoples and GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy.  The project sets a 
target of 58  per cent  of direct beneficiaries  being Indigenous Peoples;  thus the project has 
potential benefits including the promotion of Indigenous knowledge systems. Information on 
Indigenous Peoples and Afro -Mexican communities is mainstreamed throughout the project’s 
ESMF , focusing on strengthening their territorial governance and participation through the 
provision of technical support, demand -driven incentive payments and strengthening of 
interinstitutional coordination. The AE has undertaken meaningful consultations at the design 
phase, including with I ndigenous women and youth. In line with their roles and functions, the 
Indigenous Peoples Advisory Group is available to provide advice to the AE  and executing 
entities  (EEs), and the AE is encouraged to share and disseminate good practices from project 
implementation.  
47. ESS8 : Cultural heritage.  The project is not expected to adversely affect cultural 
heritage. The ESMF’s exclusion list also states that the project will not finance “Activities that 
involve directly or indirectly the conversion, losses or major or minor adverse impacts on the 
phys ical, cultural, religious, archaeological resources or intrinsic value designated by the local, 
rural and I ndigenous population” .  
48. Sexual exploitation, sexual abuse and s exual harassment (SEAH) safeguarding . 
The project is considered by the Secretariat to  potentially face high SEAH risks and impacts,  
given the existing prevalence of gender -based violence (GBV) and social conflicts linked to non -
compensatory water extraction practices, water access difficulties and a lack of employment 
opportunities in protected natural areas , which disproportionally affect rural women, youth and 
Indigenous Peoples in the Balsas Basin. As the project aims to st rengthen local watershed 
governance and the participation of women, youth and I ndigenous Peoples at the sub -basin 
level, the AE will trigger ESS standards related to labour and working conditions and community health and safety to prevent and mitigate GBV and SEAH -related risks and impacts 
related to the project. Moreover, the AE will further identify and assess specific GBV and SEAH 
risks as part of the subproject due diligence and through a social risk assessment to be 
conducted at the community  level. Detailed SEAH risk assessment s and relevant mitigation 
measures will be required as part of the subproject -specific environmental and social 
management plan s. The AE will ensure the safety and security of women ’s groups in the target 
communities, and will find age -, gender - and culturally -appropriate ways to facilitate the 
participation of women in the project. The AE will also enhance community awareness, 
capacities and strengths of project beneficiary communities, implementing partners and 
government staff in preventing and reducing the GBV and SEAH risks, and proactively map the 
existence of and gaps in services for survivors/affected pe ople . In the case of an SEAH inciden t, 
the project will follow the reporting and response protocols already established by the AE that 
include  specific SEAH -related procedures to provide essential services and redress to


---
*Page 112*
---

 
GCF/B.3 9/02/Add. 11 
Page 112 
 
 
survivors/affected pe ople . SEAH cases can be reported through the project ’s grievance redress 
mechanisms, and SEAH complaints can also be received through the AE’s complaints procedure 
to be forwarded to the AE ’s Ethics Office.  
49. Implementation arrangements.  CONAFOR as the EE will have the overall operational 
responsibility in implementing the project. A  project coordination unit will be established, 
based at CONAFOR ’s headquarters , which will be responsible for day-to-day project 
management, coordination, implementation, and monitoring including on environmental and 
social performance. The unit will coordinate the screening of subprojects, assessment and 
management of potential risks and impacts, as well as conduct stakeholder engagement, 
capacity -building an d disclosure of information. The AE will also provide training to the 
relevant staff on safeguards -related topics when the project is launched  and will continue to 
provide support as necessary.  
50. Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure.  In preparation for the project, 
the AE has conducted consultations and workshops with representatives from national 
ministries, regional organizations, multisectoral bodies, I ndigenous Peoples, as well as local 
government  organization s and the private sector. A stakeholder e ngagement plan outlines the 
processes and mechanisms for continued consultations with different stakeholders,  including 
disadvantaged and vulnerable groups , during project implementation . Engagement approaches 
will include consultations, meetings, workshops, distribution of accessible materials/leaflets on 
project activities and local radio, among others.   
51. Grievance redress mechanism.  The ESMF describe s the existing institutional level 
grievance mechanism (the Citizen Attention Mechanism) that is in place at CONAFOR for 
complaints that may be received by the project. It also describes the AE’s grievance mechanism 
including a mechanism for handl ing SEAH  grievances. These will be communicated to the 
organizations and beneficiaries of the project by the EEs. In line with the GCF Indigenous 
Peoples Policy, the GCF I ndigenous Peoples Focal Point will be available for assistance at any 
stage, including before a claim has been made.  
4.2 Gender policy  
52. The AE has provided a gender analysis and action plan , and therefore complies with the 
GCF G ender Policy. The project is designed to stimulate climate investments that contribute to 
reducing gender gaps and promote equality through climate- change mitigation and adaptation 
measures. Based on the findings of the gender analysis, recommendations were formulated in to 
a gender action plan (GAP) to ensure women ’s participation in project activities and benefits by 
defining participation targets and capacity -building for gender equality for the different groups 
of project stakeholders. The GAP is elaborated on the basis of the project ’s expected results and 
outputs framework and defines activities, indicators and targets for women ’s participation 
along with an estimated budget.  
53. The GAP aims to strengthen women ’s participation in decision -making, promote greater 
gender equity in institutions, meaningfully include women as beneficiaries of investment plans, 
build capacity through training on gender and inclusion, and use selection criteria for p ayment 
of environmental services projects that favo ur wome n’s participation. Additionally, the project 
will build women’s capacity to strengthen the systems of the farming communities, be trained in 
production practices and technologies adapt ed to climate change and variability, and benefit 
from the financial transfers made by the project. Climate -resilient sustainable forest 
management activities will open opportunities for women to participate in value chains, such as 
those linked to the sustainable use of and value addition to timber and non- timber forest 
products while also allowing the  exchange of experiences and good practices implemented by 
women in the project.


---
*Page 113*
---

 
GCF/B.3 9/02/Add. 11 
Page 113 
 
 
54. The AE is strongly encouraged to both measure and promote women -led and managed 
proposals and projects related to integrated land and water resources management . This would 
further align with the aim of the project to strengthen wome n’s leadership and further minimize 
the risk of women being marginalized.  
4.3   Risks  
4.3.1.  Overall programme assessment (medium risk)   
55. This funding proposal is a cross -cutting project ( 78 per cent adaptation and 22  per cent 
mitigation) to streng then the climate resilience of vulnerable communities and reduce 
emissions in the Balsas Basin in Mexico. To achieve its objectives, the project aims to implement 
a climate -sensitive ecosystem management  system , a climate -resilient productive system , and a 
climate information and risk management system in the country. GCF is considering a loan of 
USD 20 million and a grant of USD 19.5 million togeth er with co -financing of USD 51.5 million in 
loan and grant from IFAD  and the Government of Mexico . The co -financing ratio is 1:1.3 and 
accounts for 5 7 per cent of total financing.  
4.3.2.  Accredited entity /executing entity capability to execute the current programme 
(low risk)  
56. IFAD is an international AE with over 40 years of development work in global 
agriculture and food security. To date, IFAD has provided over USD 23 billion in grants and low -
interest rate loan s to fund livelihood s in fragile and developing countries. The project ’s EEs are 
HACIENDA, NAFIN , and CONAFOR. As the focal point for international climate finance in the 
country, Ministry of Finance and Public Credit  will sign the subsidiary loan agreement with 
IFAD , and NAFIN will act as the inter mediary  and fiduciary of the project loans. Responsibility 
for project execution will rest with CONAFOR , which also manages the countr y’s National Forest 
Fund. Since its creation in 2001, CONAFOR has developed a track record of working on 
internationally funded forestry projects and program mes. CONAFOR has worked with IFAD for 
over 15 years , including on the implementation of loans and grants for forest areas and 
vulnerable population s. 
4.3.3.  Project -specific execution risks ( medium risk)  
57. In addition to the three E Es mentioned above  (HACIENDA, NAFIN and CONAFOR) , 
decisions are overseen by a steering committee that reports to political decision  makers who 
approve the working plan and select implementation partners. CONAFOR is one of five steering 
committee members. Under the steering committee are two layers of technical coordination 
team s and units wh ich provide technical advice . These are followed by regional committees and 
local co -implementing partners , and at the bottom are the grass -root s organizations, 
communities and forestry enterprises. Given the multilayered and cross -institutional 
arrangement, operational risk and potential delays due to poor coordination is a concern. This 
risk is partly mitigated by the project ’s initiative to strengthen intersectoral coordination and 
regular engagement with stakeholders. The project will also benefit from the support of the 
Office for Coordination of Advisers and HACIENDA. The funding proposal presents low credit 
risk given the investment grade BBB rating of Mexico as the debtor . Although  a portion of the 
funding will be denominated in U nited States dollars to fund local  currency generating activities, 
foreign currency risk is likewise limited bec ause this will be backstopped by the government ’s 
investment grade rating. With regard to the risk of local currency devaluation after 
disbursement affecting the project budget, this will be managed by planning currency 
conversion as close as possible to expected disbursement .


---
*Page 114*
---

 
GCF/B.3 9/02/Add. 11 
Page 114 
 
 
4.3.4.  Project viability and concessionality  
58. The proposed project will develop plans to integrate climate change into government 
program mes to improve capacity and strengthen coordination. Other components will enhance 
sustainable forest landscapes while enabling timely climate information availability to manage 
climate risk. All these initiatives are well within the expertise of the AE and E Es given their track 
record in this space , and the project is expected to be viable.  The requested participation from 
GCF will represent approximately 43 per cent  of total programme size in a project funded partly 
by loan s and partly by grant s. The level of concessionality of one of the loans appears high in 
view of a n economy as  creditworthy and large as  that of  Mexico. However, it  is intended to be 
blended to make a forest payment of environmental services scheme viable over a 40 -year 
period. The lower concessionality loan is in tended for revenue -generating activities that are 
more economic ally viable but that would not have been funded otherwise.  
4.3.5.  Compliance risk ( medium risk)   
59. Mexico faces a number of challenges in terms of internal conflict and social unrest. This 
is largely  due to the presence of drug cartels and criminal gang violence , including in the 
targeted region . The proposal by the A E (IFAD)  does address this issue and sets out measures to 
address and mitigate any consequences or impacts. The AE has also created  its program me to 
assure appropriate financial integrity practices, such as preventing money -laundering , terrorist 
financing and prohibited practices. For any proposed disbursement of funds or commodities to beneficiaries directly, IFAD has established clear guidelines as to eligibility and me asures to 
monitor an d prevent abuse. Having cons idered the context in which the proposal will be 
operating , and the measures  propose d by the AE, a medium rating is applied.  
4.3.6.  GCF portfolio concentration risk ( low risk)   
60. In case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio concentration in 
terms of results area and single proposal is not material.
